                                          Composite witness sieves, Kneser–Plancherel containers, and the
                                                                   exponent 5/7
                                                         for the Erdős–Straus conjecture
                                                                                   Benjamin Dahan

                                                                                  November 9, 2026
arXiv:2611.08717v1 [math.NT] 5 Nov 2026




                                                                                        Abstract
                                                 For the Erdős–Straus equation 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z, the strongest unconditional upper
                                             bound on the exceptional set E(N ) of insoluble integers n ≤ N has remained since Vaughan
                                             (1970) at |E(N )| ≪ N exp(−c(log N )2/3 ). In a companion paper [1], we proved that any
                                             large-sieve scheme restricted to prime divisor witnesses p | (an + u) with p ≡ −1 (mod 4ua) is
                                             universally capped at this 2/3 exponent by the τ3 divisor barrier, whereas retaining composite
                                                                                                                 4
                                             witnesses s | (an + u) at fixed search depth J requires a 2Θ(J ) subset enumeration that
                                                                                             λ
                                             breaks down at polynomial depth J = (log N ) (λ > 1/3). In the present paper, we harness
                                             composite divisor witnesses at growing depth to establish two unconditional bounds beyond
                                             Vaughan’s 2/3 barrier. First, on the full exceptional set E(N ), we prove the unconditional
                                             bound
                                                                                                  (log N )5/7
                                                                                                               
                                                                         |E(N )| ≪ N exp −c′                      ,
                                                                                                (log log N )2/7
                                             improving the exponent from 2/3 to 5/7 (with an intermediate stage at 7/10). Second, on
                                             the structured small-sheet component E2small (N ; J(N )) (and across the certified container
                                             hierarchy E2cert (N ; (log N )λ ) for all λ ∈ (1/3, 1/2)), we combine an algebraic and spectral
                                             container hierarchy with a generating-polynomial large sieve to reach the exponent 1 − o(1):

                                             ation cost in Gu,a = (Z/4uaZ)× , the accumulation of off-kernel prime collisions, and the
                                             presence of unlocalized diffuse divisor sheets. In the first method (Sections 2–6), after es-
                                             tablishing modulator-free quadratic duality, global Jacobi reciprocity, and dihedral cofactor
                                             rigidity on the Type I/Type II variety, we replace subgroup generation by Kneser’s multi-
                                             plicative stabilizer K(M ) = StabGu,a (Σ(M )). Because K(M ) embeds into the kernel of a

      bound along an infinite sequence of primes. Appendix A establishes unconditional sieved
      candidate abundance, divergence of the square-free main term via the Rosser–Iwaniec linear
      sieve, and complete finite square-divisor positivity certificates (b | h2 ). These main-term esti-
      mates do not by themselves imply positivity of the divisor-encounter count: the Transversal
      Hypothesis T and the signed spectral error targets (C17) and (C76) remain open, and condi-
      tional finiteness holds only beyond a threshold N∗ supplied by that hypothesis, which is not
      determined by the ambient factorial volume calculation.


1     Introduction
1.1   Background and the two barriers of Paper I
The Erdős–Straus conjecture [2] asserts that for every integer n ≥ 2, the rational number 4/n can
be expressed as a sum of three positive unit fractions:
                                    4   1 1 1
                                      =  + +                  (x, y, z ∈ N).                                (1)
                                    n   x y z

By elementary reductions going back to Mordell [4], it suffices to verify (1) for primes n ≡ 1
(mod 24). Let E(N ) denote the exceptional set of integers n ≤ N for which (1) has no solution.
Since the work of Vaughan [3] in 1970, the best unconditional upper bound on |E(N )| has stood
at
                               |E(N )| ≪ N exp − c(log N )2/3
                                                               

(see also Sander [7, 8], Elsholtz–Tao [5], and Pomerance–Weingartner [6]).
    To understand why the exponent 2/3 has acted as a persistent barrier—and how composite
divisor witnesses allow one to cross it—recall the two-parameter normal form established in the
companion paper [1, Thm. 3.9] (building on the Type I/Type II classification of [5, 6]): for a
prime n ≡ 1 (mod 24), 4/n admits a Type II representation (1) if and only if there exist coprime
positive integers (u, a) such that an + u possesses a divisor

                              s | (an + u)      with        s ≡ −1   (mod 4ua).                             (2)

Dually, by [1, Thm. 3.11], 4/n admits a Type I representation if and only if there exist coprime
positive integers (u, a) with u < n such that an + u possesses a divisor s ≡ −n (mod 4ua).
For a finite family P ⊆ {(u, a) ∈ N2 : gcd(u, a) = 1}, let EP (N ) denote the set of primes
n ≤ N , n ≡ 1 (mod 24), for which no pair (u, a) ∈ P admits a divisor satisfying (2), and write
E2 (N ; J) := EP2 (J) (N ) for the full coprime box

                                                                                        6 2
                 (u, a) ∈ [1, J]2 : gcd(u, a) = 1 ,
             
P2 (J) :=                                                  P = A(J) = |P2 (J)| =           J + O(J log J).
                                                                                        π2


                                                       2
Notice that E(N ) ∩ [2, N ] is contained, up to primes ≤ 24, in E2 (N ; J) for every choice of J ≥ 1.
   In [1], two sieve methods were brought to bear on E2 (N ; J), each optimal within its regime
and each encountering a sharp structural wall:

 (1) The single-prime large sieve at growing depth and the universal τ3 ceiling. Restricting the
     witnesses s in (2) to primes s = p ∈ (J 5 , z] reduces (2) to the linear congruence n ≡
     −ua−1 (mod p) whenever 4ua | (p + 1). Applying Montgomery’s large sieve [10] as in
     Vaughan [3] yields |E2 (N ; J)| ≪ N exp − ( 2π9 2 λ2 + o(1))(log log N )3 at polynomial depth
     J = (log N )λ , and O N exp(−0.1113(log N )2/3 ) at the optimal depth log J ≍ (log N )1/3 [1,
                                                       

     Thm. 4.28]. However, [1, Thm. 4.30] proves that every single-prime congruence system
     extracted from the full Type I/Type II dichotomy     P has local witness count    bounded by
     ϱI+II (p) ≤ τ3 (p + 1) + τ3 (3p + 1). Consequently, p≤Z ϱ(p)/p ≪ (log Z) , and under the
      max                                                                         2

     large-sieve modulus constraint S(Z) log Z ≤ 21 log N , the sifting mass S(Z) cannot exceed
     O((log N )2/3 ). No sieve restricted to prime witnesses can ever surpass Vaughan’s exponent
     2/3.
                                                                              4
 (2) The composite-witness involution sieve at fixed depth and the 2Θ(J ) barrier. Retaining
     composite witnesses s = p1 · · · pr ≡ −1 (mod 4ua) changes the geometry completely. For
     any n ∈ E2 (N ; J), the set Tu,a (n) ⊆ Gu,a := (Z/4uaZ)× of prime-factor residues of an + u
     meets each orbit {x, −x−1 } of the fixed-point-free involution ι(x) = −x−1 at most once,
     so |Tu,a (n)| ≤ 21 φ(4ua) [1, Lemma 4.2]. Conditioning on the tuple of half-sized subsets
     (Tu,a )(u,a)∈P2 (J) forces n to avoid at least 12 P residue classes modulo p on average, yielding
     the exact fixed-depth sieve dimension |E2 (N ; J)| ≪J N (log N )−1−A(J)/2 [1, Thm. 4.3]. At
     polynomial depth J = (log N )λ (λ > 1/3), a sieve of dimension P/2 ≍ (log N )2λ would
     immediately surpass the 2/3 exponent. Yet as soon as exp(Gu,a ) > 2, the subgroup ⟨Tu,a (n)⟩
     generated by a half-sized subset can contain −1. In [1], this forced the enumeration of all
     half-sized subsets Tu,a ⊆ Gu,a , incurring a prohibitive combinatorial prefactor
                                                                    
                                                 Y          φ(4ua)            4
                                                                        = 2Θ(J )
                                       
                               C P2 (J) =
                                                           φ(4ua)/2
                                           (u,a)∈P2 (J)


     that overwhelms the large-sieve saving whenever J ≫ (log log N )1/2 .

   The central objective of this paper is to unlock composite divisor witnesses at growing depth
and surpass Vaughan’s 2/3 barrier unconditionally. Doing so requires overcoming three distinct
obstructions:
               4
  (i) The 2Θ(J ) subset-enumeration and collision barrier: replacing arbitrary subsets Tu,a ⊆ Gu,a
      by low-complexity containers without paying |I|R to locate off-container prime factors;
                                                       

 (ii) The unlocalized diffuse-sheet obstruction: controlling primes n for which the divisor residue
      set Σ(an + u) ⊆ Gu,a has intermediate density without concentrating in a subgroup coset
      or a heavy Fourier mode;

(iii) The CRT capacity ceiling: optimizing the trade-off between sifting dimension, collision tol-
      erance, and the Bombieri–Vinogradov / large-sieve modulus budget up to N 1/2−ε .

   We resolve these obstructions through two complementary composite-witness architectures:

   • Unconditional exponent 5/7 on the full exceptional set E(N ) (Section 7). To bypass the un-
     localized diffuse sheets completely without enumerating divisor containers in Gu,a , we con-
     struct a family of semi-composite witnesses s = pq ≡ −1 (mod 12ua), where p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z]
     is a large prime and q ≤ Q is a rough composite cofactor. Conditioning on b = n mod Msmall
     renders the witness events over p mutually independent in the Chinese Remainder Theorem


                                                  3
      model, while the progression modulus 12ua ≤ 12Z 1/4 governing p is completely indepen-
      dent of q. Coupling a polynomial Bonferroni majorant with the Siegel-zero-free Bombieri–
      Vinogradov theorem breaks Vaughan’s 1970 barrier unconditionally on the entire exceptional
      set E(N ):                                           !
                                             (log N )5/7
                    |E(N )| ≪ N exp −c′                        (Theorem 7.12),
                                           (log log N )2/7

      with an intermediate stage giving the exponent 7/10 at Q = exp(t1/3 ) (Theorem 7.11).

   • Unconditional exponent 1 − o(1) on the structured component E2small (N ; J(N )) via Kneser
     and Markov–Plancherel containers (Sections 3–6). On the full coprime box P2 (J) at poly-
     nomial depth J = (log N )λ (λ ∈ (1/3, 1/2)), we replace subgroup generation by a hierar-
     chy of Kneser stabilizer kernels, Markov 1/2-popular symmetry sets, quotient containers,
     and Pontryagin–Plancherel character containers, coupled with a Krawtchouk–Montgomery
                                                                     4
     generating-polynomial large sieve. This eliminates
                                                   √         the 2Θ(J ) barrier, closes the full set
     E2 (N ; J) unconditionally up to J = exp(c0 log log N ) and for λ ≤ 1/78, surpasses the 2/3
     exponent across λ ∈ (1/3, 1/2) on the certified container hierarchy E2cert (N ; (log N )λ ) (Theo-
     rem 6.10), and reaches the exponent 1 − o(1) on the small-sheet component E2small (N ; J(N ))
     up to the CRT capacity scale logloglogNN of the generating-polynomial construction:
                                                                                                   

1.2   Summary of main results
We now summarize the architecture and principal theorems of the paper, which are organized into
seven interconnected parts; Table 1 at the end of this subsection provides a synoptic comparison
of the quantitative bounds and their exact scopes.

1. Modulator-free duality, global Jacobi reciprocity, and dihedral rigidity (Section 2).
We begin by establishing exact algebraic and quadratic-reciprocity obstructions that constrain the
prime factors of an + u and Kc = (n + c)/4 well beyond the single involution ι1 (x) = −x−1 :

   • Modulator-free quadratic duality (Theorem 2.1, Corollary 2.2, and Proposition 2.3): 4/n
     admits a Type II decomposition if and only if there exist positive integers α, u with n ∤ αu
     such that n+4αu2 possesses a divisor s ≡ −1 (mod 4αu) (equivalently c ≡ −n (mod 4αu)),
     via the exact identity sc = (4aαu − 1)(4αuv − n) = n + 4αu2 and its Dirichlet character
     expansion.

   • Global Jacobi reciprocity on the Type II
                                              variety (Theorem 2.4): every Type II witness obeys
     the global reciprocity laws n = s = −1, n = −1, and αc
                                   s       n            uv
                                                                             = −1. In particular,
                                                           
                                                                         n
     this law excludes quadratic-residue classes modulo n rather than half of the divisors of each
     individual integer (Subsection 2.2).

   • Cofactor dihedral rigidity and quadruple involution (Theorem 2.6 and Remark 2.7): when
     u | 6, the congruence an + u ≡ a + u =: r (mod 4ua) ensures that the cofactor map
     d 7→ (an + u)/d induces a second involution ιr (x) = −rx−1 on Gu,a . Under the unit and
     fixed-point-free hypotheses of Theorem 2.6, ι1 and ιr generate a dihedral action decomposing
     Gu,a into even cycles of length 2L = 2 ord4ua (r), reducing the count of maximum-sized
     admissible subsets from 2φ(4ua)/2 to 2φ(4ua)/(2L)−1 and locking the principal cycle onto ⟨r⟩.
     Incorporating the Type I branch adds twofurther

4. Polynomial-depth decomposition and the Two-Stage Kneser Tower (Section 5).
At polynomial depth J = (log N )λ (0 < λ < 1/2), Theorem 5.1 decomposes E2 (N ; J) \ [1, z]
at the collision threshold R0 ≍ (log N )2λ log log N into the Kernel / Low-Collision component
E2Ker (N ; J, R0 ) and the Scarcity component E2Scar (N ; J, R0 ):

     to avoid −ΣI1 (an + u)−1 with zero collisions on I2 . Theorem 5.2 proves that any collision
     in (−1)Hu,a on I2 reflects into Hu,a on I1 , forcing index doubling [Gu,a : K1+ (n)] ≥ 4 and
     raising the local sifting density to at least 3/4.

5. Greedy Markov containers, Quotient containers, and Pontryagin–Plancherel Char-
acter containers (Section 6). To control the Scarcity branch at growing depth, Section 6
replaces linear coset growth (+|K|) by geometric growth (×3/2) and dual Fourier containers:
   • Markov 1/2-popular symmetry containers (Lemma 6.1 and Theorem 6.2): using the sym-
     metry set S1/2 (A) = {g ∈ G : |A ∩ gA| > 21 |A|} (of size < 2|A|), a greedy multiplicative
     skeleton of length s ≤ 5 log J + 1 reduces State I collisions per pair from φ(4ua) − 2 ≍ J 2
     to 5 log J +√1, closing the Scarcity branch and the full set√E2 (N ; J) unconditionally up to
     J = exp(c0 log log N ), with |E2 (N ; J)| ≪ N exp(− exp(c log log N )).

   • Quotient containers and simultaneous smooth conditioning (Theorems 6.3 and 6.4): at poly-
     nomial depth J = (log N √) , Quotient State I is closed unconditionally for all quotients of
                               λ

     order Du,a ≤ exp(c0 (λ) log log N ), while global conditioning on n mod Q(z0 ) determines
     all smooth prime factors below z0 simultaneously with no power of P in the container count.

     6.5, Theorems 6.6–6.10, and Corollary 6.12): in the odd Hall subgroup Gu,a,imp , Lemma 6.5

6. Semi-composite congruence sieve, CRT conditional independence, and the uncon-
ditional exponent 5/7 on E(N ) (Section 7). To extend unconditional super-Vaughan decay
to the full exceptional set E(N ) without conditioning on container localization in Gu,a , Section 7

2     Modulator-free duality, global Jacobi reciprocity, and dihedral
      rigidity
Before constructing growing-depth sieves, we establish several exact algebraic obstructions on the
Type I and Type II varieties that constrain the prime factors of an + u and Kc = (n + c)/4. In [1],
the fixed-depth sieve relied solely on the direct modular involution ι1 (x) = −x−1 (mod 4ua),
treating the parameters (u, a) as independent and ignoring both the cofactor (an + u)/s and
quadratic reciprocity modulo n. Tracking these additional symmetries reveals three structural
obstructions: a modulator-free quadratic factorization, a global Jacobi reciprocity law modulo n,
and a dihedral action generated by the cofactor map.

2.1     Self-admissible characterization via n + 4αu2 and 4a2 αn + 1
In [1, Thm. 3.9], the Type II criterion was parameterized by the coprime pair (u, a) through
the divisibility s | (an + u) with s ≡ −1 (mod 4ua), where a = (u + v)/c is the modulator
and Kc = (n + c)/4 = αuv. Eliminating either the pair (u, v) or the pair (a, v) yields two
dual characterizations in which the trivial divisors 1 and n + 4αu2 are automatically excluded by
congruence modulo 4.

Theorem 2.1 (Modulator-free quadratic duality). Let n ≡ 1 (mod 24) be prime. The following
statements are equivalent:

    (i) 4/n admits a solution of Type II;

 (ii) there exist positive integers a, α ∈ N∗ such that 4a2 αn + 1 possesses a divisor s ≡ −1
      (mod 4aα);

(iii) there exist positive integers α, u with n ∤ αu such that n + 4αu2 possesses a divisor s ≡ −1
      (mod 4αu);

 (iv) there exist positive integers α, u with n ∤ αu such that n + 4αu2 possesses a divisor c ≡ −n
      (mod 4αu).

In (iii) and (iv), the trivial divisors 1 and n + 4αu2 are excluded modulo 4, and the complementary
factors are linked by the identity

                           s · c = (4aαu − 1)(4αuv − n) = n + 4αu2 ,                            (3)

where c = (n + 4αu2 )/s ≡ 3 (mod 4) is the shift, a = (s + 1)/(4αu) ∈ N∗ is the modulator, and
v = (n + c)/(4αu) ∈ N∗ is the complementary divisor of [1, Thm. 3.9].

Proof. Step 1: Equivalence (i) ⇔ (ii). By [1, Thm. 3.9], 4/n admits a Type II solution if and
only if there exist a, α, u, v ∈ N∗ satisfying an = 4aαuv − u − v. Multiplying both sides by 4aα
and adding 1 rewrites this relation as

                               (4aαu − 1)(4aαv − 1) = 4a2 αn + 1.

Conversely, if s | (4a2 αn + 1) satisfies s ≡ −1 (mod 4aα), then its cofactor s′ = (4a2 αn + 1)/s
satisfies ss′ ≡ 1 (mod 4aα), and hence s′ ≡ −1 (mod 4aα). Since 4aα ≥ 4, neither s nor s′ equals
1, so u = (s + 1)/(4aα) ≥ 1 and v = (s′ + 1)/(4aα) ≥ 1 are positive integers.



                                                9
Step 2: Implication (i) ⇒ (iii). Given a Type II representation with parameters (a, α, u, v), set
s = 4aαu − 1 ≡ −1 (mod 4αu) and c = (u + v)/a = 4αuv − n ∈ N∗ . The modulator a cancels in
the product sc:

        s · c = (4aαu − 1)c = 4αu(ac) − c = 4αu(u + v) − (4αuv − n) = n + 4αu2 .

Hence s | (n + 4αu2 ) with s ≡ −1 (mod 4αu). Furthermore, the positive normal form gives
n = 4αuv − (u + v)/a ≥ 2uv and n ≥ 4α − 2, which implies 1 ≤ u, v, α < n. Since n is prime, it
follows that n ∤ αu.
Step 3: Equivalence (iii) ⇔ (iv). Put m = 4αu. Since n is prime with n ≡ 1 (mod 4) and n ∤ αu,
both n and n + 4αu2 are coprime to m. Every divisor of n + 4αu2 is therefore a unit modulo m,
so for any factorization sc = n + 4αu2 we have

                            s ≡ −1      (mod m)    ⇐⇒      c ≡ −n       (mod m).

Step 4: Implication (iii) ⇒ (i). Let s | (n + 4αu2 ) satisfy s ≡ −1 (mod 4αu), and define
the cofactor c = (n + 4αu2 )/s. Since n + 4αu2 ≡ 1 (mod 4) and s ≡ 3 (mod 4), we have
s∈ / {1, n + 4αu2 } and c ≡ 3 (mod 4) with c ≥ 3. Because c ≡ −n (mod 4αu), the quantities
a := (s + 1)/(4αu) ≥ 1 and v := (n + c)/(4αu) ≥ 1 are positive integers. A direct expansion then
gives
                         (s + 1)(n + c) − 4αu2 − (n + c)   sn + sc − 4αu2   (s + 1)n
  4aαuv − u − v =                                        =                =          = an,
                                       4αu                      4αu           4αu
which yields a Type II solution by [1, Thm. 3.9].

Corollary 2.2 (Primary filter on n + 4). If n + 4 possesses a divisor s ≡ 3 (mod 4), then 4/n ad-
mits a Type II solution with u = α = 1, modulator a = (s+1)/4, and shift c = (n+4)/s. In partic-
ular, the divisor s = 7 | (n + 4) resolves the four exceptional primes n ∈ {409, 577, 23929, 1134241}
recorded in [1, Prop. 3.7].
   Passing to the multiplicative group (Z/4αuZ)× , Theorem 2.1(iii) allows us to express the
number of Type II witnesses at fixed (α, u) by character orthogonality, isolating a systematic
vanishing of real characters with χ(n) = −1.
Proposition 2.3 (Character formula for n + 4αu2 ). Fix a prime n ≡ 1 (mod 24) and positive
integers α, u with n ∤ αu, and set M (α, u) := n + 4αu2 . The number NII (n; α, u) of divisors
s | M (α, u) satisfying s ≡ −1 (mod 4αu) is given by

     map d 7→ M (α, u)/d induces the functional equation τχ (M (α, u)) = χ(n)τχ (M (α, u)). In

Proof. Since n is prime with n ≡ 1 (mod 4) and n ∤ αu, we have gcd(M (α, u), 4αu) = 1. Or-
thogonality of Dirichlet characters modulo 4αu gives (4), and complete multiplicativity yields the
Euler product for τχ .
    For part (a), if χ is real, each local Euler factor 1 + χ(q) + · · · + χ(q)e equals e + 1 ≥ 1 when
χ(q) = 1, and equals 1 (for e even) or 0 (for e odd) when χ(q) = −1; hence τχ (M (α, u)) ≥ 0.

When χ = χ and χ(n) = −1, this forces τχ (M (α, u)) = −τχ (M (α, u)) = 0.
    For part (b), since n ≡ 1 (mod 3), the residues 3 − n ≡ 6 − n ≡ 2 (mod 3) are coprime to 3.
If 3 ∤ αu, then 4αu2 ≡ α (mod 3), so when α ≡ 2 (mod 3) the two lifts 3 − n and 6 − n modulo 9
are attained by two of the three values α + 3b (b ∈ {0, 1,p2}). For these values, v3 (M (α, u)) = 1, so
M (α, u) is not a square and pairing each divisor s < M (α, u) with M (α, u)/s gives the stated
sum.

2.2   Global Jacobi reciprocity law on the Type II variety
Beyond modular congruences modulo 4ua, the bilinear structure of the Type II equation imposes
a global quadratic reciprocity invariant modulo n on every witness s, divisor pair (u, v), and shift
c.

 (a) (Jacobi invariance on 4uaα − 1). For any positive integers u, a, α ∈ N∗ , set s = 4uaα − 1.
     Then the Jacobi symbols satisfy

     and u + v = ac, one has

     uv possesses at least one prime factor q ≥ 5 of odd multiplicity with nq  = −1. Moreover,

     in the segmented sieve of [1, Alg. 2] any candidate divisor s ≡ 3 (mod 4) with ns = +1 is
     obstructed.

Remark 2.5 (Scope of the Jacobi obstruction). Theorem 2.4(b) shows that any Type II witness
s must be a quadratic non-residue modulo n. This is a global reciprocity constraint modulo n;
for a fixed integer an + u, it does not by itself force half of the divisors of an + u to have Jacobi
symbol +1 and half −1.

2.3   Cofactor dihedral rigidity, quadruple involution, and global vacuity of
      quadratic kernels
In [1, Lemma 4.2], the upper bound |Tu,a (n)| ≤ 12 φ(4ua) on the set Tu,a (n) ⊆ Gu,a = (Z/4uaZ)×
of prime-factor residues of an + u used only the direct involution ι1 (x) = −x−1 (mod 4ua). When
u | 6, the residue of M = an + u modulo 4ua is fixed independently of n ≡ 1 (mod 24): tracking
the cofactor M/d of each divisor d | M then introduces a second involution ιr (x) = −rx−1 that
pairs with ι1 to generate a dihedral action on Gu,a .

Theorem 2.6 (Cofactor dihedral rigidity). Let n ≡ 1 (mod 24) be prime, let u ∈ {1, 2, 3, 6}, and
let a ≥ 1 satisfy gcd(u, a) = 1, a + u odd, and n > u. Set M = an + u and G = (Z/4uaZ)× .

 (a) One has M = an + u ≡ a + u (mod 4ua) with gcd(a + u, 4ua) = 1, so r := (a + u) mod 4ua
     is a fixed element of G independent of n.

 (b) If M has no divisor s ≡ −1 (mod 4ua), then the prime-factor residue set T = Tu,a (n) ⊆ G
     avoids both singletons −1 and −r, and meets each orbit of ι1 (x) = −x−1 and each non-
     singleton orbit of the cofactor involution ιr (x) = −rx−1 at most once.

 (c) Suppose that r = a + u ≡ 3 or 5 (mod 8). Then both ι1 and ιr are fixed-point-free on G, and
                                                                              φ(4ua)
     the incompatibility graph generated by ι1 and ιr partitions G into m = 2 ord 4ua (r)
                                                                                          even cycles
     of length 2L, where L = ord4ua (r). On the principal cycle C1 = ⟨r⟩ ⊔ (−⟨r⟩), the unique
     admissible subset of maximal size L is the cyclic subgroup ⟨r⟩, and the number of admissible
     subsets of cardinality 21 φ(4ua) in G satisfies
                                                          φ(4ua)
                                                                      −1
                                     Nmax (u, a) ≤ 2 2 ord4ua (a+u)        .                     (7)

Proof. Proof of (a) and (b). Since u | 6 and n ≡ 1 (mod 24), we have 4u | 24 | (n − 1), so
4ua | a(n − 1) and an + u = a(n − 1) + a + u ≡ a + u (mod 4ua). Because a + u is odd and
gcd(u, a) = 1, we have gcd(a + u, 4ua) = 1, proving (a). For (b), if −r ∈ T , then some prime
q | M satisfies q ≡ −r (mod 4ua), and its cofactor s = M/q | M satisfies s ≡ r(−r)−1 ≡ −1
(mod 4ua), a contradiction. Similarly, if x ∈ T and ιr (x) = −rx−1 ∈ T are distinct residues,
they are carried by distinct primes q1 , q2 | M , and the cofactor s = M/(q1 q2 ) | M satisfies
s ≡ r(x(−rx−1 ))−1 ≡ −1 (mod 4ua).
Proof of (c). Since a + u is odd and u ∈ {1, 2, 3, 6}, exactly one of u, a is even, so 8 | 4ua. When
r ≡ 3 or 5 (mod 8), the residue −r ≡ 5 or 3 (mod 8) is not a square modulo 8, which ensures

                                                 12
that ιr is fixed-point-free on G. Because (ιr ◦ ι1 )(x) = rx, each connected component of the graph
with edges {x, ι1 (x)} and {x, ιr (x)} is of the form x⟨r⟩ ∪ (−x−1 ⟨r⟩). For any x ∈ G, the cosets
x⟨r⟩ and −x−1 ⟨r⟩ are disjoint: an intersection would give −x−2 ∈ ⟨r⟩, whereas modulo 8 we have
−x−2 ≡ 7 (mod 8) and ⟨r⟩ mod 8 = {1, r mod 8} ⊆ {1, 3, 5}. Each component is therefore a cycle
of even length 2L.
    On the principal cycle C1 = (v0 , v1 , . . . , v2L−1 ) with v2k = rk and v2k+1 = −r−k , deleting the
two forbidden neighbours v1 = −1 and v2L−1 = −r of v0 = 1 leaves the isolated vertex v0 = 1
together with an odd-length path (v2 , . . . , v2L−2 ) on 2L−3 vertices. The unique independent set of
size L − 1 on this path consists of the even-indexed vertices {v2 , v4 , . . . , v2L−2 } = {r, r2 , . . . , rL−1 }.
Thus T ∩ C1 = ⟨r⟩ is uniquely fixed, while each of the m − 1 remaining bipartite cycles of length
2L admits at most 2 independent sets of size L, giving (7).

Remark 2.7 (Scope of the maximal dihedral count and extensions). The bound (7) counts
admissible subsets of exact cardinality |G|/2; a smaller admissible subset need not embed into an
admissible subset of size |G|/2. The same dihedral mechanism extends to two further settings:

 (1) Modulator-free family M (α, u) = n+4αu2 . Since M (α, u) ≡ n (mod 4αu) and −1 ≡ −n ≡ 3
     (mod 4), both ι1 (x) = −x−1 and ιn (x) = −nx−1 (mod 4αu) are fixed-point-free for every
     pair (α, u) with n ∤ αu. The number of admissible subsets of size 21 φ(4αu) is therefore at
                                    φ(4αu)
                                             −1
      most Nmax (α, u; n) ≤ 2 2 ord4αu (n)        .

 (2) Quadruple Type I+II involution. Suppose u ∈ {1, 3}, a ≡ u (mod 4), gcd(u, a) = 1, n > u,
     and gcd(n, 4ua) = 1. Since 8u | 24 | (n − 1), the integer M ′ = (an + u)/2 satisfies
     M ′ ≡ r′ := (a + u)/2 (mod 4ua) with gcd(M ′ , 4ua) = 1. If (u, a) produces neither a Type II
     witness (s ≡ −1) nor a Type I witness (s ≡ −n), then Tu,a (n) avoids the four singletons
     {−1, −r′ , −n, −n−1 r′ } (mod 4ua) and meets each orbit of ι1 (x) = −x−1 , ιr′ (x) = −r′ x−1 ,
     τn (x) = −nx−1 , and τnr′ (x) = −n−1 r′ x−1 at most once. Note that if Tu,a (n) ⊆ ker χ
     for a quadratic character χ, multiplicativity forces χ(r′ ) = 1, whereas the triple condition
     χ(−1) = −1, χ(r′ ) = 1, χ(n) = 1 characterizes quadratic kernels that are themselves
     half-sized sets admissible under all four involutions.

     At (u, a) = (1, p) with p ≡ 5 (mod 8) prime and np = −1, this quadruple action forces

   Even for Type II witnesses alone, complete multiplicativity obstructs half of all quadratic
character kernels whenever u is 3-smooth and a + u is not a square modulo 4ua.

Theorem 2.8 (Multiplicativity obstruction for quadratic kernels). Let n ≡ 1 (mod 24) be prime,
and let (u, a) be a coprime pair with n > u.

 (a) (Elimination of ker χ4 ). If a + u ≡ 3 (mod 4), then an + u ≡ 3 (mod 4), so an + u possesses
     at least one prime factor q ≡ 3 (mod 4). Consequently, the kernel ker χ4 = {x ∈ Gu,a : x ≡ 1
     (mod 4)} never contains Tu,a (n) for any prime n ≡ 1 (mod 24).

  (b) (Obstruction for 3-smooth u). For all u, a ≥ 1, one has an + u ≡ a + u (mod 24a). If
      u = 2α 3β (α, β ≥ 0) is 3-smooth and a + u is odd, then the primitive conductor of every real

      Dirichlet character χ (mod 4ua) divides 24a, so χ(an + u) = χ(a + u) is independent of n.
      In particular, for every quadratic character χ (mod 4ua) satisfying χ(a + u) = −1, the set

      of r in G/G2 is trivial, is independent of the class of −1, or coincides with the class of −1.
Proof. For (a), n ≡ 1 (mod 4) implies an + u ≡ a + u ≡ 3 (mod 4), which cannot be a product
of primes congruent to 1 (mod 4).
     For (b), writing n = 24k + 1 gives an + u = 24ak + (a + u) ≡ a + u (mod 24a). The primitive
conductor of any real Dirichlet character modulo 4ua has 2-part dividing 8 and squarefree odd
part. When u = 2α 3β is 3-smooth, every odd prime dividing 4ua divides 3a, so the primitive
conductor divides 24a, and therefore χ(an + u) = χ(a + u). If Tu,a (n) ⊆ ker χ, then χ(q) = +1
for every prime q | (an + u) (as gcd(an + u, 4ua) = 1), which by complete multiplicativity forces
χ(an + u) = +1, contradicting χ(a + u) = −1. Finally, X = Hom(G/G2 , {±1}) is the dual of the

of size |X|/2 gives 0, |X|/4, or |X|/2 solutions according as r ∈ G2 , rG2 ∈       / {G2 , −G2 }, or

3     The Kneser Stabilizer–Kernel Dichotomy and 2-Sylow reduction
We now address the main combinatorial bottleneck of the fixed-depth composite-witness sieve
of [1, §4.1]. For a prime n ∈ E2 (N ; J) and a coprime pair (u, a) ∈ P2 (J), the set Tu,a (n) ⊆
Gu,a = (Z/4uaZ)× of prime-factor residues of an + u satisfies |Tu,a (n)| ≤ 12 φ(4ua), because
Tu,a (n) meets each orbit {x, −x−1 } at most once. However, as soon as exp(Gu,a ) > 2, a subset of
size 12 φ(4ua) avoiding −1 need not generate a proper subgroup: the subgroup ⟨Tu,a (n)⟩ can equal
all of Gu,a and contain −1. To retain arbitrary divisor products without subgroup closure, [1] had
to enumerate all half-sized subsets of Gu,a across P2 (J), incurring a combinatorial prefactor of
          φ(4ua)     Θ(J 4 ) that prevented J from growing as a power of log N .
Q
   (u,a) φ(4ua)/2 = 2
                            4
    To eliminate this 2Θ(J ) factor, we replace the subgroup ⟨Tu,a (n)⟩ generated by the prime
factors with the multiplicative Kneser stabilizer K(M ) = StabGu,a (Σ(M )) of the full divisor-
residue set Σ(M ) of M = an + u. Because 1 ∈ Σ(M ) and −1 ∈          / Σ(M ), the subgroup K(M )
is contained in Σ(M ) and therefore always avoids −1. Every subgroup avoiding −1 embeds
into a maximal such subgroup, which we show is the kernel of a 2-primary Dirichlet character
                                                                                                4
ψ (mod 4ua) with ψ(−1) = −1. This reduces the container family across P2 (J) from 2Θ(J )
arbitrary subsets to exp(O(J 2 log log J)) character kernels, while Kneser coset growth bounds the
number of prime divisors of an + u falling outside the kernel.

3.1   2-Sylow classification of maximal subgroups avoiding −1
We first classify the maximal subgroups of a finite abelian group G that avoid a given element
ϵ ∈ G of order 2, showing that they correspond bijectively to cyclic quotients of the 2-Sylow
subgroup G2 .

3.2   The Stabilizer–Kernel Theorem and Kneser coset growth
We now connect the divisor set Σ(M ) of an integer M having no divisor s ≡ −1 (mod 4m) to the
character kernels of Lemma 3.1. The key observation is that while individual prime divisors of M
may lie outside the stabilizer K(M ) = StabG (Σ(M )), each such prime divisor strictly increases
the number of K(M )-cosets in the partial subproduct chain.

     from Σ(M ), improving the upper bound to [G : K(M )] − 3.

 (c) (Kernel–Scarcity dichotomy). Put L(M ) = ⟨q mod 4m : q | Mcop ⟩. Exactly one of the
     following two alternatives holds:

        • Kernel case (−1 ∈
                          / L(M )): there exists H ∈ M(G, −1) containing L(M ), so that
          K(M ) ⊆ Σ(M ) ⊆ L(M ) ⊆ H and ΩG\H (M ) = 0.
        • Scarcity case (−1 ∈ L(M )): every H ∈ M(G, −1) containing K(M ) satisfies

                                                 |Σ(M )|

                                                 |K(M )|

     and M ≡ 1, 3, or 5 (mod 8)), any prime integer Mcop (Ω(Mcop ) = 1) satisfies −1 ∈ / L(M )
     and falls into the first alternative.

Remark 3.3 (High-multiplicity residue classes).     P Let e = exp(G) denote the exponent of G =
(Z/4mZ)× , and let T (≥e−1) (M ) := {x ∈ G : pk ∥M, p≡x(4m) k ≥ e − 1} denote the set of residue
classes carrying at least e − 1 prime factors of M counted with multiplicity. Because any class
x ∈ T (≥e−1) (M ) realizes all powers 1, x, . . . , xe−1 mod 4m as coprime divisor residues of M , the
subgroup H ∗ (M ) := ⟨T (≥e−1) (M )⟩ ≤ G is contained in Σ(M ) and avoids −1. Consequently,
T (≥e−1) (M ) is always contained in a maximal kernel H ∈ M(G, −1) with zero off-kernel collisions.

3.3     Average 2-Sylow enumeration cost across the full coprime box P2 (J)
By Lemma 3.1(c), each pair (u, a) ∈ P2 (J) admits at most 12 φ2 (4ua) maximal subgroups avoiding
−1. Bounding 21 φ2 (4ua) pointwise by 2J 2 would give a product of (2J 2 )P = exp(O(P log J))
kernel tuples across P2 (J). We now show that because log2 ( 21 φ2 (4ua)) decomposes as a sum of
two additive arithmetic functions of u and a, its average over P2 (J) is only (4 + o(1)) log log J,
reducing the global container count to exp(O(P log log J)).

4     The    Imperfect-Avoidance                      and           Generating-Polynomial
      Large Sieves
In the classical large sieve framework of [1, §4.3], the sifted set E ⊆ [1, N ] avoids the forbidden
residue classes Ωp ⊆ Z/pZ completely at every prime p ∈ I. By contrast, Theorem 3.2 shows that
for a fixed kernel tuple Ψ = (Hu,a )(u,a)∈P , an exceptional prime n = 24k + 1 ∈ EP (N ) may still
meet the forbidden classes Ωp (Ψ) up to R times across p ∈ I. A naive union bound over all |I|
                                                                                                    
                                                                                                  R
possible collision subsets would reintroduce a prohibitive combinatorial factor. In this section, we
establish two generalizations of Montgomery’s large sieve to imperfect avoidance (≤ R collisions)
that absorb the collision exceptions algebraically into the sieve weights, completely avoiding any
 |I|
  R subset enumeration.


4.1   Montgomery’s large sieve with imperfect avoidance via Krawtchouk iden-
      tities
We first treat the regime of bounded sieve degree h, where the homogeneous elementary symmetric
functions of the local sifting weights expand into an exact Krawtchouk polynomial identity in the
number of collisions.

4.2     The Krawtchouk–Montgomery generating-polynomial large sieve

   More generally, the generating-polynomial large sieve yields a non-trivial exponential saving

Proposition 4.4 (Subcritical collision closure). Under the notation of Theorem 4.3, suppose

5      Polynomial-depth                decomposition                and        the        Two-Stage
       Kneser Tower
We now pass from logarithmic depths to polynomial search depths J= (log N )λ with 0 < λ <
1/2, where the number of coprime pairs is P = A(J) = π62 + o(1) (log N )2λ and the typical
group order    φ(4ua) ≍ J 2 = (log N )2λ is exponentially larger than the logarithmic sifting window
      log z 
log log J 5 ≍ log log N . Consequently, the worst-case Kneser bound φ(4ua) − 2 of Theorem 3.2
no longer fits deterministically below 14 Smin .
    To analyze this regime, we introduce two structural devices: first, we partition E2 (N ; J) \ [1, z]
at the subcritical global collision threshold R0 (λ, N ) ≍ (log N )2λ log log N into a low-collision
kernel component E2Ker (N ; J, R0 )—which is unconditionally closed with exponent 2λ surpassing
2/3 for all λ ∈ (1/3, 1/2)—and a high-collision scarcity component E2Scar (N ; J, R0 ); second, we
split the sifting interval into two stages I1 = (J 5 , z1 ] and I2 = (z1 , z], converting first-stage prime
collisions into a large divisor-residue set ΣI1 (an + u) whose inverse −ΣI1 (an + u)−1 must be
avoided with zero collisions on the second stage I2 .

5.2   Inside/Outside duality and index doubling in the Two-Stage Kneser Tower
On a two-stage split I1 = (J 5 , z1 ] and I2 = (z1 , z], the zero-collision avoidance law (34) creates a
duality between the exterior of the kernel Hu,a (n) on the second stage and the interior of Hu,a (n)
on the first stage. Specifically, any prime factor of an + u on I2 lying in the opposite coset
(−1)Hu,a (n) maps under the involution d 7→ −d−1 mod 4ua to a residue class inside Hu,a (n) that
must be missed by the first-stage internal divisor set Σ+      1 (n) ⊆ Hu,a (n). By Kneser’s theorem
applied inside the ambient group Hu,a (n), this forces the internal stabilizer K1+ (n) to be a proper
subgroup of Hu,a (n), doubling its index in Gu,a to at least 4.

Theorem 5.2 (Two-stage descending Kneser tower). Let J 5 < z1 < z, I1 = (J 5 , z1 ], and
I2 = (z1 , z]. Fix n ∈ E2 (N ; J) \ [1, z] and (u, a) ∈ P2 (J), and let H = Hu,a (n) = ker ψu,a ∈
M(Gu,a , −1) be a maximal kernel avoiding −1. Define the first-stage component internal to H:

5.3    Rigorous realization of the two-regime survival model
In [1, §4.5], an unproved heuristic model for the one-parameter survival curve J 7→ |E1 (N ; J)|
isolated two distinct regimes: a first regime (u ≲ log n) governed by ≍ 2ω(2u) index-2 subgroup
obstructions of density 1/2, predicting a survival rate exp − ( 21 log log n − (log 2) log log J)J
that [1, Theorem 4.3] could not reach at growing depth due to the subset-enumeration factor
                4
C(P) = 2Θ(J ) , and a second regime (u ≫ log n) governed by divisor scarcity (τ (n + u) ≪ φ(4u)).
     The results of Sections 3–5 turn this two-regime picture into unconditional theorems in both
                                                                                                        4
one and two parameters. First, Theorem 3.2 and Lemma 3.4 replace the subset count 2Θ(J )
                                                       2
by the 2-primary kernel count C ∗ (P2 (J)) ≤ 2(4+o(1))J log log J (and C ∗ (P1 (J)) ≤ 2(2+o(1))J log log J
in one parameter), exhibiting the subtractive term −O(log log J) against the main sifting gain
                                                                                            log z
+ 12 log log N . Second, Theorems 4.6 and 4.8 prove that whenever φ(P) ≤ 18 log log            p0 , the
scarcity regime is empty. Third, at polynomial depth J = (log N ) , Theorems 5.1 and 5.2 close
                                                                      λ

the low-collision kernel branch while trapping the scarcity branch in a descending Kneser tower.


6     Greedy Markov containers, quotient containers, and Plancherel
      character containers
In Sections 3–5, bounding the number of prime factors outside the exact stabilizer K(M ) =
StabG (Σ(M )) relied on the linear growth of the number of K(M )-cosets (|Σ
                                                                          e j | ≥ |Σ
                                                                                   e j−1 |+|K(M )|),
which yields the worst-case bound ΩG\K(M ) (M ) ≤ φ(4ua) − 2 ≍ J . At super-polylogarithmic
                                                                     2

or polynomial depths J = (log N )λ , a collision bound of order J 2 per pair exceeds the available
sifting mass ≍ (1 − 2λ) log log N .

The geometry of the M -stage dyadic escalation and why it crosses λ = 1/3 without
violating the parity barrier. Before constructing the container hierarchy, let us explain the
two structural mechanisms that allow us to cross Vaughan’s single-prime threshold λ = 1/3
(exponent 2λ = 2/3) on the certified container hierarchy without encountering Selberg’s parity
barrier:

    1. Bypassing the parity barrier via two-stage bilinear divisor synthesis. Unlike a linear sieve
       attempting to isolate prime values of an + u, our upper-bound sieve exploits the absence of
       composite divisors s ≡ −1 (mod 4ua) of an + u. Splitting the sifting interval I = (J 5 , z]
       into two stages I1 = (J 5 , z1 ] and I2 = (z1 , z], every divisor d | MI1 (an + u) formed by
       products of first-stage prime factors creates a forbidden residue class −d−1 mod 4ua on the
       second stage I2 : if a prime p ∈ I2 divides an + u and satisfies p ≡ −d−1 (mod 4ua), then
       gcd(p, d) = 1 (since d is z1 -smooth and p > z1 ), so the composite product s = pd | (an + u)
       satisfies s ≡ −1 (mod 4ua) and resolves 4/n directly.
      Consequently, an + u must avoid the entire inverted divisor sheet −ΣI1 (an + u)−1 ⊆ Gu,a
      on I2 with strictly zero collisions (RII = 0). The parity obstruction is bypassed because the


                                                   29
     witness s = pd is synthesized bilinearly from arbitrarily many primes in I1 coupled with a
     single prime p ∈ I2 .

  2. The M -stage dyadic escalation 0 = η0 < η1 < · · · < ηM . In a single-stage container
     scheme on Gu,a /Ku,a of order Du,a ≤ φ(4ua) ≍ (log N )2λ , reaching the dense threshold
     |Σ1 (n)| > 14 Du,a in one leap from A0 = {1} requires either enumerating a multiplicative
     skeleton of length s ≍ log Du,a at cost Du,a  s    = exp(Θ((log log N )2 )), or tolerating up to
              log Du,a
     Ru,a ≲ log(4/3)   ≍ 2λ log log N Plancherel collisions, which becomes supercritical against the
                              I

                                                                                                 2
     To break this bottleneck for λ ∈ (1/3, 1/2), we introduce a fine exponent step ε1 = (1−2λ)

Why does independent per-pair skeleton enumeration in the full group Gu,a cost (4J 2 )s =

entropic barrier via two complementary mechanisms: passing to quotient groups Gu,a = Gu,a /Ku,a
(Theorem 6.3), and conditioning globally on n mod Q(z0 ) across all P pairs simultaneously (The-
orem 6.4).

6.4   Total maximal subgroups, Cauchy–Davenport growth, and Pontryagin–
      Plancherel character containers in the odd Hall subgroup
By Theorem 6.3(b), the 2-Sylow projection of ΣI1 (an + u) saturates Gu,a,2 across almost all pairs
(u, a). We now analyze the remaining configurations in the odd Hall subgroup Gu,a,imp (and in
the full group Gu,a ) by passing to the Pontryagin dual G
                                                        b u,a .

Taxonomy of the structured exceptional subsets E2small (N ; J), E2cert (N ; J), and E2diffuse (N ;
J). To clarify which subset of E2 (N ; J) \ [1, z] is bounded by each theorem at polynomial depth
J = (log N )λ (λ ∈ (1/3, 1/2)), we distinguish three nested or complementary components accord-
ing to the structure of the first-stage divisor sheets ΣI1 (an + u) ⊆ Gu,a across (u, a) ∈ P2 (J):
   • The small-sheet structured component E2small (N ; J): the set of exceptional primes n ∈
     E2 (N ; J) \ [1, z] for which a macroscopic proportion of pairs (u, a) ∈ P2 (J) (at least P/2
     pairs in Theorem 6.10, or at least P/4 pairs in Corollary 6.12) have a sparse first-stage
     divisor sheet |ΣI1 (an + u)| ≤ φ(4ua)ε1 . Intuitively, for n ∈ E2small (N ; J), the prime factors
     of an + u in I1 fail to expand the divisor-residue set beyond the initial polynomial threshold
     φ(4ua)ε1 .
      By Theorem 6.8(a)–(b), both the Markov 3/2-growth step count and the adaptive Plancherel
      collision count are strictly subcritical with effective enumeration cost ≤ 4φ(4ua)ε1 per pair.
      This component is bounded unconditionally by ≪ N exp(−c(λ)(log N )2λ log log N ) in The-
      orem 6.10(a) and reaches the exponent 1 − o(1) in Corollary 6.12.
   • The certified structured container hierarchy E2cert (N ; J): the larger subset of E2 (N ; J) \ [1, z]
     consisting of all primes n that either belong to the low-collision Kernel branch E2Ker (N ; J, R0 )
     (bounded in Theorem 5.1(a)) or belong to the Scarcity branch with at least P/4 pairs (u, a) ∈
     P2 (J) falling into one of the four algebraically or spectrally certified container regimes (i)–
     (iv) detailed in Theorem 6.10(a): (i) sub-threshold divisor sheets |ΣI1 (an + u)| ≤ φ(4ua)ε1
     (which contains E2small (N ; J)), (ii) proper subgroup concentration (ωGu,a \ker χ (an + u; I1 ) <
     r1 for some χ ̸= χ0 ), (iii) subcritical Plancherel spectral buckets ℓ ≤ ℓsub (λ) or Bohr-localized
     ℓ = 1 harmonic dilation cones, and (iv) quotient-certified escalation to State II in a quotient
     Gu,a /Ku,a with Ku,a ≤ StabGu,a (ΣI1 (an + u)).
     Theorem 6.10(a) bounds the entire certified hierarchy E2cert (N ; J) unconditionally by ≪
     N exp(−c(λ)(log N )2λ log log N ) for all λ ∈ (1/3, 1/2).
   • The residual unlocalized diffuse component E2diffuse (N ; J) := E2 (N ; J) \ [1, z] \ E2cert (N ; J):

    We also emphasize that the CRT capacity statement in Corollary 6.12(c) concerns the specified

7    Semi-composite    congruence    sieve,   CRT     conditional
     independence, and the unconditional exponent 5/7
In Section 6, Theorem 6.10 and Corollary 6.12 surpassed Vaughan’s 2/3 exponent unconditionally
on the certified structured hierarchy E2cert (N ; J) and reached the exponent 1 − o(1) on the small-
sheet component E2small (N ; J(N )). However, those bounds apply to the structured components
where the first-stage divisor sheet ΣI1 (an + u) ⊆ Gu,a is either sub-threshold, concentrated near a
proper subgroup, localized in a subcritical Plancherel bucket or harmonic cone, or certified in a low-
order quotient. Extending an unconditional super-Vaughan bound to the full global exceptional

                                                       49
set E(N ) requires eliminating the residual unlocalized diffuse component E2diffuse (N ; J) without
enumerating divisor sheets in Gu,a .

Bypassing diffuse divisor sheets and decoupling the Bombieri–Vinogradov modulus
via s = pq ≡ −1 (mod 12ua). To bound the entire exceptional set E(N ) unconditionally, we
invert the perspective of Section 6: instead of constructing composite witnesses s = pd implicitly
from the unknown divisor sheet ΣI1 (an + u) of the target integer n, we prescribe a priori an
explicit family of semi-composite moduli

     sα = p q ≡ −1       (mod 12ua),         p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z] prime,         q ∈ Q ⊂ [2, Q] y-rough,

with U = Z 1/10 ≤ u, a ≤ V = Z 1/8 and y = t6 (t := log Z). By Lemma 7.2, each congruence
Aα : n ≡ −ua−1 (mod pq) directly yields an Erdős–Straus decomposition of 4/n, so every excep-
tional integer n ∈ E(N ) must simultaneously avoid all progressions Aα regardless of the internal
geometry of its divisor sheets. This semi-composite construction overcomes Vaughan’s 2/3 barrier
through two coupled decoupling mechanisms:

  1. CRT factorization and conditional Bernoulli independence given b = n mod Msmall . Because
     every cofactor q ∈ Q satisfies q ≤ Q ≤ exp( c23 t1/2 ) < Z 9/10 < p, the small
                                                                               Q cofactor modulus
     Msmall = lcmq∈Q q is coprime to the large prime modulus Mlarge = Z 9/10 <p≤Z p. By the
     Chinese Remainder Theorem, the witness congruence n ≡ −ua−1 (mod pq) splits into two
     orthogonal conditions:

                      ab + u ≡ 0     (mod q)        and      n ≡ −ua−1          (mod p),

     where b := n mod Msmall . Moreover, for each fixed prime p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z], the scale hierarchy
     V 2 = Z 1/4 < Z 9/10 < p and Q < 12U 2 ≤ 12ua forces the progressions A(u,a,q,p) to be strictly
     disjoint in Z (Proposition 7.3(a)). Consequently,
                                                  P once the cofactor projection b ∈ Z/Msmall Z
     is conditioned upon, the indicators Jp (n) = (u,a,q) 1A(u,a,q,p) (n) ∈ {0, 1} become mutually
     independent Bernoulli random variables in the CRT model.

  2. Decoupling the Bombieri–Vinogradov modulus 12ua ≤ 12Z 1/4 from the cofactor  P q. 1For a
     single-prime congruence sieve (q = 1), the sifting mass up to Z is bounded by u,a φ(12ua) ≍
     t2 , which under the capacity constraint t3 ≲ log N locks the saving exponent at (log N )2/3 .
     Adjoining the y-rough cofactors q ∈ Q multiplies the conditional sifting mass µ(b) =
     ECRT [W | b] by the composite harmonic sum MQ = q∈Q φ(q)          ≍ log Q
                                                                          log t , raising the typical
                                                           P
                                                                   q2
     mean to L ≍ t2 log Q
                     log t . Crucially, this cofactor amplification does not increase the modulus in
     the prime number theorem: in the inner sum
                                                           X           1
                                   S(u, a, q) =                          ,
                                                                       p
                                                      Z 9/10 <p≤Z
                                                   pq≡−1 (mod 12ua)


     the prime p is constrained only modulo m = 12ua ≤ 12V 2 = 12Z 1/4 , which is completely
     independent of q. When gcd(q, 12ua) = 1, multiplication by q merely permutes the target
     residue class r = −q −1 mod 12ua inside (Z/12uaZ)× . After discarding at most one Landau–
     Page–Siegel exceptional prime factor pexc ≥ 5 P(Lemma 7.1), the Siegel-zero-free Bombieri–
     Vinogradov theorem bounds the discrepancy (u,a)∈P ′ E(u, a) ≪ exp(−c3 t1/2 ) uniformly
     over all residue classes coprime to 12ua prior to summing over q ∈ Q (Proposition 7.7).
                                                 t5/2                             t5/2
     Taking Q = exp( c23 t1/2 ) then yields L ≍ log t at Bonferroni degree k ≍ log t and total

7.1    Construction of the witness family s = pq and exclusion of the exceptional
       prime factor
Let Z ≥ 100 be a large real parameter, eventually chosen as a sub-exponential function of N . We
set the following scales in terms of t := log Z:

                       t := log Z,     U := Z 1/10 ,     V := Z 1/8 ,     y := t6 ,                  (75)

and we consider a truncation parameter Q for the composite cofactors satisfying
                                                   c      
                                                     3 1/2
                                exp t1/3 ≤ Q ≤ exp                                                   (76)
                                        
                                                       t     ,
                                                     2
where c3 > 0 denotes the absolute constant provided by the Bombieri–Vinogradov theorem after
excluding the exceptional modulus (Proposition 7.7 below).
    In order to obtain an error term of size exp(−c3 t1/2 ) in the distribution of primes p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z]
in arithmetic progressions modulo 12ua ≤ 12V 2 = 12Z 1/4 , we first discard a potential prime factor
of the Landau–Page–Siegel exceptional conductor.

Lemma 7.1 (Exclusion of a Landau–Page–Siegel exceptional        √ prime factor). Let t := log Z and
set the Landau–Page–Siegel conductor scale T0 := exp(a0 t), where a0 > 0 is a small absolute
constant. By the Landau–Page theorem (see [21, Chap. 14]), there exists an absolute constant
c1 > 0 such that, among all primitive real Dirichlet characters χ of conductor qχ ≤ T0 , there is
at most one character χexc (of conductor qexc ≤ T0 ) whose L-function L(s, χexc ) has a real zero
β > 1 − logc1T0 = 1 − a √clog

                       0


 (a) If such a character χexc exists, its conductor qexc has at least one odd prime factor pexc :=

     0).

 (b) For every pair of integers (u, a) satisfying pexc ∤ ua, the modulus m = 12ua satisfies pexc ∤ m,

                                                        √       qexc ≤ T0 . Applying the Landau–

                             0
to a unique primitive real character χexc of conductor qexc ≤ T0 (while primitive characters of
conductor qχ > T0 are handled by Vinogradov’s bilinear method in the Bombieri–Vinogradov
theorem; see [21, Chap. 28] or [22, Prop. 9.2]).
Step 2: Existence of an odd prime factor pexc ≥ 5 and non-divisibility of 12ua. Since χexc is a
primitive real character, its conductor qexc is the absolute value of a fundamental discriminant:
in particular, v2 (qexc ) ∈ {0, 2, 3} and the odd part of qexc is squarefree (so v3 (qexc ) ∈ {0, 1}). If
all prime factors of qexc belonged to {2, 3}, then qexc would divide 24. However, there are only
finitely many real characters of conductor dividing 24, and their L-functions have no real zeros
in [1 − a √clog
             1
                Z
                  , 1] once Z is sufficiently large. Thus we necessarily have qexc > 24, which ensures
         0
that qexc possesses at least one odd prime factor pexc := P + (qexc ) ≥ 5. Finally, since pexc ≥ 5 is
coprime to 12, the condition pexc ∤ ua implies by Euclid’s lemma that pexc ∤ 12ua. Since pexc | qexc ,
it immediately follows that qexc ∤ 12ua.

Lemma 7.2 (Arithmetic validity of the semi-composite witness congruences). For every quadruple
α = (u, a, q, p) ∈ IZ :

 (a) The modulus sα = pq satisfies gcd(pq, 12ua) = 1, and in particular gcd(a, pq) = 1 (so that
     the inverse a−1 mod pq exists) and gcd(pq, 24) = 1.

 (b) Every integer n ≥ 1 belonging to the residue class Aα satisfies the Egyptian fraction identity
     with strictly positive integer denominators:
       4     1    1     1                                       pq + 1                  an + u
         =     +     +      ,              with          v :=          ∈ N≥1 ,   d :=          ∈ N≥1 . (81)
       n   3uvd 3avnd 3avnu                                      12ua                     pq

      Consequently, every exceptional integer n ∈ E(N ) simultaneously avoids all residue classes
      Aα for α ∈ IZ .

Proof. Step 1: Proof of (a). By Bézout’s identity, the congruence pq ≡ −1 (mod 12ua) implies
gcd(pq, 12ua) = 1. In particular, gcd(a, pq) = 1, and since pq ≡ −1 (mod 12), pq is odd and not
divisible by 3, whence gcd(pq, 24) = 1.
Step 2: Proof of (b). By definition of IZ , pq + 1 is a strictly positive multiple of 12ua, so
v = pq+1
     12ua ∈ N≥1 . If an integer n ≥ 1 satisfies n ≡ −ua
                                                        −1 (mod pq), then multiplying by a yields

an + u ≡ 0 (mod pq). Since a, n, u ≥ 1, the integer an + u is strictly positive and divisible by
pq, which guarantees that the quotient d = an+u  pq  belongs to N≥1 . We then have the algebraic
identity:
                           an + u + d = pq d + d = (pq + 1)d = 12uavd.
Dividing both sides by the strictly positive integer 3uavnd, we obtain:
      an + u + d     an     u       d       1    1     1     12uavd  4

where the three denominators 3uvd, 3avnd, and 3avnu belong to N≥1 .

7.2    Disjointness for a fixed large prime factor p and conditional independence
       in the CRT model
Let us set:
                                                         Y
              Msmall := lcmq∈Q q,       Mlarge :=                 p,     MZ := lcmα∈IZ sα .            (82)

For sufficiently large Z, every cofactor q ∈ Q satisfies q ≤ Q ≤ exp( c23 t1/2 ) < Z 9/10 < p for every
prime p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z]. Consequently, all prime factors of Msmall are ≤ Q < Z 9/10 , whereas all those
of Mlarge are > Z 9/10 : we therefore have gcd(Msmall , Mlarge ) = 1, and MZ | Msmall Mlarge .

Proposition 7.3 (Disjointness for fixed p and CRT conditional independence). For sufficiently
large Z:

 (a) (Disjointness for a fixed large prime p). Let p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z] be a fixed prime. If two witnesses
     α = (u, a, q, p) ∈ IZ and α′ = (u′ , a′ , q ′ , p) ∈ IZ sharing the same large prime factor p have
     a non-empty intersection (Aα ∩ Aα′ ̸= ∅), then α = α′ .

 (b) (Reduction to Bernoulli random variables). For each prime p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z], set

      Then, for every integer n ∈ Z, we have Jp (n) ∈ {0, 1}, and the total number of witness
      congruences satisfied by n can be written as:

 (c) (Conditional mutual independence in the CRT model). Set M := Msmall Mlarge (so that MZ |
     M        and        every Q congruence          Aα        is      M-periodic),         equip
             ∼
     Z/MZ = (Z/Msmall Z) × Z 9/10 <p≤Z (Z/pZ) with the uniform probability measure PCRT
     (whose marginal on Z/MZ Z is also uniform), and let b := n mod Msmall denote the reduction
     of n modulo the set of cofactors. Conditioned on any fixed configuration b ∈ Z/Msmall Z, the

Proof. Step 1: Uniqueness of the pair (u, a) modulo p. Suppose there exists n ∈ Z belonging
simultaneously to Aα and Aα′ , where α = (u, a, q, p) and α′ = (u′ , a′ , q ′ , p) share the same prime
p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z]. Reducing the congruences n ≡ −ua−1 (mod pq) and n ≡ −u′ (a′ )−1 (mod pq ′ )
modulo p yields:

                 −ua−1 ≡ −u′ (a′ )−1     (mod p)         ⇐⇒      ua′ − u′ a ≡ 0   (mod p).

However, by definition of P ′ , we have 1 ≤ U ≤ u, a, u′ , a′ ≤ V = Z 1/8 , which implies:

                       |ua′ − u′ a| < max(ua′ , u′ a) ≤ V 2 = Z 1/4 < Z 9/10 < p.

Since the only multiple of p with absolute value strictly less than p is 0, we obtain ua′ − u′ a = 0,
                ′
that is, ua = ua′ . As the fractions are irreducible (gcd(u, a) = gcd(u′ , a′ ) = 1) and have positive
terms, we deduce that (u, a) = (u′ , a′ ).
Step 2: Uniqueness of the cofactor q modulo 12ua and proof of (a)–(b). Since (u, a) = (u′ , a′ ),
the defining congruences (79) give pq ≡ −1 (mod 12ua) and pq ′ ≡ −1 (mod 12ua). Because
gcd(p, 12ua) = 1, p is invertible modulo 12ua, so cancelling p yields:

                                         q ≡ q′     (mod 12ua).

Yet for sufficiently large Z, the bound (76) gives q, q ′ ≤ Q ≤ exp( c23 t1/2 ) < 12Z 1/5 = 12U 2 ≤ 12ua.
Two positive integers strictly less than 12ua and congruent modulo 12ua are necessarily equal:
q = q ′ , and therefore α = α′ , proving (a). Consequently, for any fixed p ∈ (Z 9/10 , Z], the sets
A(u,a,q,p) indexed by (u, a, q) are pairwise disjoint in Z, so at most one term in the sum (83) can
equal 1, forcing Jp (n) ∈ {0, 1} and proving (b).


                                                     53
Step 3: CRT splitting and conditional mutual independence given b. For each quadruple α =
(u, a, q, p) ∈ IZ , we have gcd(p, q) = 1 (since q ≤ Q < Z 9/10 < p). By the Chinese Remain-
der Theorem, the congruence n ≡ −ua−1 (mod pq) is equivalent to the conjunction of the two
congruences:
                      ab + u ≡ 0 (mod q)       and    n ≡ −ua−1 (mod p),
where the first condition depends only on the projection b = n mod Msmall (since q | Msmall ),
while the second depends only on n mod p. When the configuration b ∈ Z/Msmall Z is fixed,
let Tp (b) denote the set of triples (u, a, q) such that (u, a, q, p) ∈ IZ and ab + u ≡ 0 (mod q).
By Steps 1 and 2, the residues {−ua−1 mod p : (u, a, q) ∈ Tp (b)} are pairwise distinct in Z/pZ:
two distinct triples in Tp (b) either have distinct pairs (u, a) ̸= (u′ , a′ ) yielding distinct residues
modulo p, or share the same pair (u, a), which uniquely determines q ≡ −p−1 (mod 12ua) in
[2, Q]. Thus, conditioned on b, Jp (n) is the indicator Q  of the event that n mod p belongs to a
subset of Z/pZ of cardinality |Tp (b)|. Since gcd(Msmall , p p) =Q1, the conditional distribution of
(n mod p)Z 9/10 <p≤Z given b is the uniform product measure on Z 9/10 <p≤Z (Z/pZ). The variables
(Jp )Z 9/10 <p≤Z are therefore mutually independent conditioned on b, each following a Bernoulli
distribution with parameter pp (b) = |Tp (b)|/p.

7.5    Exact arithmetic dichotomy and uniformity in b of the pair count (u, a)

Proposition 7.8 (Arithmetic dichotomy on gcd(b, q)). For sufficiently large Z, uniformly for
every cofactor q ∈ Q and every configuration b ∈ Z:
 (a) (Identical vanishing when gcd(b, q) > 1). If gcd(b, q) > 1, then no pair (u, a) ∈ P ′ simulta-
     neously satisfies gcd(q, 12ua) = 1 and ab + u ≡ 0 (mod q). In particular:

                               gcd(b, q) > 1       =⇒     Xqfull (b) = Yqfull (b) = 0.

 (b) (Asymptotics and uniform lower bound when gcd(b, q) = 1). If gcd(b, q) = 1, then:

Proof. Step 1: Proof of (a) (Identical vanishing when gcd(b, q) > 1). Suppose gcd(b, q) > 1 and let
r be a prime divisor of gcd(b, q). If a pair (u, a) satisfies ab + u ≡ 0 (mod q), then r | q | (ab + u).
Since r | b, the prime r divides ab, and by subtraction r must divide u = (ab + u) − ab. Thus r is
a common prime factor of q and u, which contradicts the condition gcd(q, 12ua) = 1. Both sums
in (94) are therefore empty.
Step 2: Proof of (b) — Möbius expansion and high-divisor truncation. Suppose now that gcd(b,
q) = 1. By definition of Q, every prime factor of q exceeds y = t6 > 3, so gcd(q, 12) = 1.
Furthermore, for any integer a with gcd(a, q) = 1, any integer u satisfying the congruence u ≡ −ab
(mod q) automatically satisfies gcd(u, q) = gcd(−ab, q) = 1 since gcd(b, q) = 1. The condition
gcd(q, 12ua) = 1 is therefore equivalent to gcd(a, q) = 1.

8    Exact bilinear Type II dispersion and Kloosterman reciprocity
To complement the container sieves of Sections 3–6 and prepare the study of higher polynomial
depths J = (log N )λ (λ ≥ 1/2), we record the exact, false-positive-free four-parameter bilinear
identity for Type II witnesses and its Poisson–Kloosterman reciprocity formulas.



                                                    65
8.1   False-positive-free 4-parameter bilinear identity n + 4u2 v = w(4uav − 1)
In the monomial equation 4uavα = an + u + v of [1, Thm. 3.9], a naive change of variables v ′ = av
drops the divisibility condition a | (u + v) and introduces false positives. Writing v = ac − u (or
d = aw − u for the cofactor d = (an + u)/s) resolves the divisibility a | (u + v) identically and
yields a false-positive-free bilinear equivalence:

Proposition 8.1 (Exact false-positive-free bilinear equivalence for Type II witnesses). Let n ≡ 1
(mod 24) be a positive integer and let u, a ≥ 1 be coprime positive integers. The following three
statements are strictly equivalent (with zero false positives):

  (i) the integer an + u possesses a divisor s ≡ −1 (mod 4ua);

 (ii) there exist positive integers v, w ≥ 1 such that

                   n + 4u2 v = w (4uav − 1)              ⇐⇒      n + w = 4uv (aw − u);        (108)

(iii) there exists an integer v ≥ 1 such that 4uav − 1 ≤ an + u and

                                      n ≡ −4u2 v         (mod 4uav − 1).                      (109)

Writing c = w and α = v, the bilinear identity always implies c ≡ −n (mod 4uα) and 4u2 α ≡ −n
(mod c). Conversely, suppose that u, α, c ≥ 1, c ≡ 3 (mod 4), and gcd(c, 4uα) = 1. Under these
assumptions the two congruences are equivalent to

                            n + 4u2 α + c
                     a :=                 ∈ N≥1 ,        n + 4u2 α = c(4uαa − 1).
                                4uαc
To recover the coprime-pair specialization of this proposition, one must also retain the condition
gcd(a, u) = 1 on the reconstructed integer a.

Proof. (i) ⇐⇒ (ii). If s | (an + u) with s ≡ −1 (mod 4ua), write s = 4uav − 1 (v ≥ 1) and
an + u = s d. Reducing modulo a, since an + u ≡ u (mod a) and s ≡ −1 (mod a), the quotient
d > 0 satisfies d ≡ −u (mod a), hence writes d = aw − u with w ≥ 1. Expanding

                 an + u = (4uav − 1)(aw − u) = 4ua2 vw − 4u2 av − aw + u,

subtracting u, and dividing by a ≥ 1 gives (108). Conversely, any solution (v, w) ∈ (N≥1 )2 of (108)
satisfies aw − u = n+w
                   4uv > 0; multiplying by a and adding u recovers an + u = (4uav − 1)(aw − u).
    (i) ⇐⇒ (iii) and Chinese Remainder reconstruction. Since gcd(a, 4uav − 1) = 1, the congru-
ence (109) is equivalent upon multiplication by a to

                 an ≡ −4u2 av = −u(4uav − 1) − u ≡ −u                (mod 4uav − 1),

that is, (4uav − 1) | (an + u). Finally, for the Chinese Remainder statement, c ≡ −n (mod 4uα)
and 4u2 α ≡ −n (mod c) mean that both 4uα and c divide n + 4u2 α + c; under gcd(c, 4uα) = 1,
                                                           2 α+c
this is equivalent to 4uαc | (n + 4u2 α + c), i.e. a = n+4u
                                                         4uαc    ∈ N≥1 .

Having established in Section 6 that the multiplicative container hierarchy and the Subcritical

and of its shift slices KII (c) = (n + c)/4 and KI (c) = (nc + 1)/4. To guide the reader, this section
is organized into five thematic parts:

 (1) Subsection 9.1: three-term phase separation, toric slice geometry on Vn , and L2 -dispersion
     along the Farey diagonal (Theorems 9.1, 9.2, and 9.3);

 (2) Subsection 9.2: difference-set positivity duality on D(Kc ) = Σ(Kc ) · Σ(Kc )−1 , unconditional
     (log P )4 cumulative main-term growth, and exact cancellation of small-LCM divisor pairs
     (Theorems 9.4 and 9.5);

 (3) Subsection 9.3: the cyclic quotient parity identity, the Bohr–Siegel antipodal obstruction
     certificate, the Linnik–Xylouris witness-size lower bound, and the Transversal Spectral Pos-
     itivity Hypothesis T (Theorems 9.6 and 9.7);

     orem 9.14).

9.1     Phase separation, toric geometry, and Farey-diagonal L2 -dispersion on Vn
Because the leading degree-4 monomial 4uavw of F (u, a, v, w) in (113) is the product of all four

Theorem 9.2 (Archimedean slice integral and cofactor congruence on Vn ). Let n ≡ 1 (mod 24)
be a fixed prime and let Vn ⊂ A4 be the affine threefold (113).
 (a) (Box volume and archimedean slice integral on Vn ). For any dyadic box B(U, A, V, W ) =
     [U, 2U ] × [A, 2A] × [V, 2V ] × [W, 2W ] intersecting Vn (R>0 ) in the dominant regime 4u2 v ≤ n,
     the defining polynomial F (u, a, v, w) = 4uavw − 4u2 v − w = n locks the box volume to

                                  Z 2U Z 2A Z 2V

                     IB (n) :=                                                  dv da du.        (119)

      For U, A, V ≥ 1, one always has IB (n) ≪ 1. If the subset of (u, a, v) satisfying the dis-
      played constraint has measure at least δU AV , then IB (n) ≫ δ. Thus IB (n) ≍ 1 requires a
      uniform nondegeneracy condition on the slice, not merely nonempty intersection. Restricted
      to u, a, v, w ≥ 1 and 4u2 v ≤ n, the total archimedean integral is ≍ (log n)3 . This is an
      archimedean statement on the dominant region, not a pointwise asymptotic for the number
      of integer solutions.

     2M ã with ã ∼ A/(2M0 ) (note that this progression lies in the odd-a sector and is separate
     from the even-a counting functional used in Theorem 9.1). On this progression, the integer
     an + u is deterministically divisible by M , and every solution (4uav − 1)(aw − u) = an + u
     induces a factorization M = m m′ with m = gcd(M, 4uav − 1) and m′ | (aw − u). Along
     an ≡ −u (mod m′ ), the divisor condition m′ | (aw − u) is equivalent to the shift congruence

                          m′ | (aw − u)        ⇐⇒       w ≡ −n       (mod m′ ).               (120)

     For each counted solution, m = gcd(M, s) specifies its branch uniquely, and a general un-
     conditional upper bound for (121) is ≪ε (A/M + 1)(An + u)ε . By contrast, the expression
     τ (M )/(8uM ) is only the value suggested by multiplying independent-density heuristics; it
     is not an established asymptotic for this fixed-n count, and yields no general impossibility
     theorem for progression conditioning.
Proof. (a) On Vn ∩B(U, A, V, W ) with 4u2 v ≤ n, we have 4uavw = n+4u2 v+w ≍ n, proving (118).
On [U, 2U ] × [A, 2A] × [V, 2V ] with U, A, V ≥ 1, 4uav − 1 ≍ U AV , so IB (n) ≪ 1, and IB (n) ≫ δ
whenever the slice constraint holds on a subset of measure ≥ δU AV . Restricted to u, a, v, w ≥ 1
                                           √
and 4u2 v ≤ n: for the upper bound, u ≪ n, v ≪ n, and a ≪ n, and integration of 1/(4uav−1) ≪
1/(uav) suffices; for the lower bound, restrict to 1 ≤ u, a, v ≤ n1/8 , so that w = (n+4u2 v)/(4uav−
1) ≥ 1 and 4u2 v ≤ n for large n, where the integral of 1/(4uav − 1) is ≫ (log n)3 .
   (b) Since an ≡ −u (mod M ) and m′ | M , multiplying aw − u by n modulo m′ gives

              n(aw − u) = (an)w − un ≡ −uw − un = −u(w + n)                (mod m′ ).

Because gcd(un, m′ ) = 1, m′ | (aw − u) holds if and only if w ≡ −n (mod m′ ), establishing (120).
Combining w ≡ −n (mod m′ ) with w ≡ −n (mod 4u) (gcd(4u, m′ ) = 1) gives a single residue
class modulo 4um′ with W/(4um′ ) + O(1) representatives in [W, 2W ]. Finally, for each a in the
progression a ≡ −un (mod 2M ) (≤ A/(2M ) + 1 values), the number of divisors d | (an + u) is
τ (an + u) ≪ε (An + u)ε , giving the unconditional bound ≪ε (A/M + 1)(An + u)ε .

    Finally, averaging over n ∼ N at power depth S = 4U AV ≤ N 1/2−ε introduces a fifth variable
n of volume N in which the three-term phase decomposition (115) is strictly linear, collapsing the
L2 -variance onto the exact Farey diagonal.

9.2   Difference-set positivity duality on D(Kc ), cumulative (log P )4 main term,
      and small-LCM cancellation
Whereas a single progression an + u at fixed (u, a) searches for a divisor s ∈ Σ(an + u) hitting −1
(mod 4ua) among 2ω(an+u) square-free subproducts, passing to the shift parametrization Kc =
(n + c)/4 (c ≡ 3 (mod 4), gcd(c, Kc ) = gcd(c, n) = 1) of [1, Prop. 2.4] acts on the difference set

                          D(Kc ) := Σ(Kc ) · Σ(Kc )−1 ⊆ Gc := (Z/cZ)× .

This difference set contains the square-free core D(rad(Kc )) of up to 3ω(Kc ) ternary combinations
  qiεi (εi ∈ {−1, 0, +1}) and enables direct coupling with the unconditional short-interval second-
Q
moment inequality at fixed n, as well as with the Matomäki–Radziwiłł short-interval theorem [16].

    We now record three fundamental properties of the centered spectral decomposition (129):
the unconditional (log P )4 growth of the cumulative main term across Type I and Type II shifts
KI (c) = (nc + 1)/4 and KII (c) = (n + c)/4, the exact shift-wise cost of taking absolute values
prior to summing over c, and—most importantly for the logic of the method—an exact cancellation
theorem showing that a sub-family of divisor pairs with small least common multiple carries a
divergent main term of order (loglog H
                                  log n)3/2
                                            while producing strictly zero modular encounters.

modulo c equals r. Its Fourier transform is |FK (χ)|2 − m and its total mass is m(m − 1). Since
m ≥ 2, at least one integer value h(r) is ≥ 1; Fourier inversion gives |h(r) − Mc | ≤ Bc , whence

gives (136) (including ℓ = 1, which contributes 0). For every (d, e) ∈ Bj (p), we have d, e ≤ T and
0 < d + e ≤ 2T < p (since η < 12 and p > 2H), so no modular encounter d + e ≡ 0 (mod p) can
occur. Character orthogonality applied to Bj (p) therefore gives

the signed sum.

Theorem 9.7 (The Linnik–Xylouris witness-depth lower bound, pairwise shift coprimality, and
the Transversal Hypothesis T).
 (a) (A Type II witness-size lower bound along an infinite sequence). For each integer Y ≥ 3,
     put L(Y ) = 4 lcm(1, . . . , Y ) (so 24 | L(Y )) and let nY be the least prime congruent to 1
     modulo L(Y ). The Linnik–Xylouris theorem [18] gives nY ≪ L(Y )5 , hence nY −→ ∞ and
     log nY ≤ (5 + o(1))Y . Every Type II solution at nY satisfies uv > Y , and every Type II
     witness s | (anY + u) with s ≡ −1 (mod 4ua) satisfies s > Y . Thus, along an infinite
     sequence of distinct primes,
                                                              
                                                       1
                                    min(u · v, s) ≥      − o(1) log nY .                     (145)
                                                       5
     Under GRH, nY ≪ L(Y )2 (log L(Y ))2 gives the constant 1/2 in place of 1/5. (These state-
     ments concern the indicated Type II witness sizes; they do not exclude arbitrary finite families
     of algebraic identities.)
 (b) (Pairwise shift coprimality across short intervals and the Transversal Spectral Positivity
     Hypothesis T). For any two distinct shifts p ̸= p′ in PNR (n) ∩ [1, 4H], any common prime
                                                       ′

     Positivity): there exists a fixed constant A > 5 such that for every sufficiently large prime
     n ≡ 1 (mod 24), there exists at least one shift p ∈ PNR (n) ∩ [1, (log n)A ] such that Kp =
     (n + p)/4 satisfies

Proof. For (a), we have nY ≥ L(Y ) +1 and      24 | L(Y ). For every odd m ≤ Y , quadratic
reciprocity and nY ≡ 1 (mod 4m) give nY = 1; also n2Y = 1. Multiplicativity treats every
                                             m

integer 1 ≤ r ≤ Y . The Type II Jacobi restrictions (Theorem 2.4(b)) imply uv, s > Y , and

which forces −1 mod p ∈ supp(e   µp ) ⊆ D(Kp ) and yields a Type II solution by Theorem 9.4(a).
Remark 9.8 (Methodological note on Hypothesis T and the threshold NT ). Writing NT for a
threshold beyond which Hypothesis T holds, three points should be kept in mind: (1) Hypoth-
esis T does not specify NT numerically, and the ambient factorial volume estimate established
in Theorem 9.10 below does not bound NT ; (2) the exponent condition A > 5 is part of the
chosen formulation of Hypothesis T, rather than a consequence of an unconditional polylogarith-
mic least-prime theorem; and (3) while prime factors q > H of Kp are pairwise disjoint across
p ∈ PNR (n)∩[1, 4H], pairwise disjointness of large prime factors does not by itself imply statistical
independence of the associated character phase tuples.
