Remark 9.11 (Methodological distinctions: ambient volume, fixed-shift saturation, and average
abundance). To keep the logical role of each estimate in Theorem 9.10 completely transparent,
we record the following structural distinctions:




                                                      89
                                                                                         |∆−
                                                                                           k (dp )|
 (1) Ambient cross-polytope volume vs. arithmetic probability: The factorial bound          dkp
                                                                                                      1
                                                                                                    ≤ k!
      in (151) and its sum Mobs (p; n) in (153) measure the normalized counting volume of the
      relaxed cone ∆−                                   − k
                       k (dp ) in the ambient product (µ2dp ) ; they do not constitute an arithmetic
      probability distribution of character phases and do not bound the threshold NT of Hypoth-
      esis T. Likewise, in part (c), the dual construction c = 4K − n is conditional on finding a
      square-free product K ∈ (n/4, n/3) of primes from Q− (n) and does not guarantee primality
      of c, while a lower bound on ω(Kp ) alone implies neither the existence of s successful Markov
      3/2-extensions nor R simultaneous character collisions across G   b−
                                                                         p \ {ψ0 }.

 (2) Parseval support and Kneser dichotomy under −1 ∈         / D(Kp ): In part (d), the diagonal
     lower bound µ  ep (1) ≥ | supp µp | yields µ
                                        −1        ep (1) ≥ |Σ(Kp )|−1 ≥ τ (Kp )−1 for any measure
     supported on Σ(Kp ), and µ   ep (1) ≥ 2 −ω(K p ) for the square-free Bernoulli measure; in the

     undersampled regime 2ω(Kp )+1 ≤ p − 1, the negation of the Bohr–Siegel certificate (143) is
     therefore necessary but not sufficient for positivity because of 2π-phase wrap-around. In the
     Kneser saturation bounds (155), (157), (158), and (159), all lower bounds that add 2|Hp | per
     off-Hp prime factor are derived under the failure hypothesis −1 ∈    / D(Kp ) and are compared
     against p − 1 − |Hp | (while for a selected divisor U | Kp , its linear Kneser contribution uses
     ΩGp \Hp (U )). Note also that whereas |Σ(Kp )| > p−1 2 suffices unconditionally for −1 ∈ D(Kp )
     by the pigeonhole principle, a lower bound |D(Kp )| > p−1      2 alone does not suffice unless
     D(Kp ) is a subgroup or avoids −1 outside a proper coset.

 (3) Average Bombieri–Vinogradov abundance vs. fixed-shift saturation and composite moduli:
     In part (e), the inequalities (157)–(159) are deterministic lower bounds at a given shift p.
     The Bombieri–Vinogradov construction over p ∈ (2H, 4H] supplies shifts where the small-
     factor counts produce bounds of size H o(1) (such as 3m ≫A (log log p)A ), which do not
     by themselves reach (p − 1)/2, nor do they imply the existence of saturating shifts in a
     smaller polylogarithmic window p ≤ (log H)C without an estimate at that smaller scale
     (note also that when H = (log n)A , log log H = log log log n + log A, so prime-factor counts
     restricted to q ≤ H β retain the scale log log H). Moreover, the unitary co-divisor property
                                                                                    √
     gcd(U, Kp /U ) = 1 is an additional hypothesis beyond U | Kp , and U ≥ 12 p − 1 allows
     4d0 u21 + 1 to reach the archimedean threshold p without by itself forcing 4d0 u21 + 1 ≡ 0
     (mod p). Finally, in (160), for a composite modulus m = 4ak, saturation estimates retain
     the actual Carmichael exponent λ(m) and Davenport constant D((Z/mZ)× ): smoothness
     of m alone does not make them sub-polynomial since λ(2r ) = 2r−2 for r ≥ 3, whereas in the
     special cases m = 4ak with ak | 6 one has λ(m) = 2.

    To make the role of the co-divisor Vp = Kp /U in (158) and of the dual inversion c = 4K − n
in Theorem 9.10(c) completely explicit, we establish two exact structural results: (i) a cofactor
factorization analysis distinguishing the global residue class of an unfactored cofactor V from the
intermediate divisor quotients of V (including an exact obstruction when V = ℓ is prime with
c > 4U 2 + 1, and a three-branch criterion when V = ℓr has two prime factors), and (ii) an
archimedean support bound showing that at large polynomial scales H = nθ with θ > 2/3, all
Type II encounters are confined to a sparse union of intervals An (H) of cardinality O((n/H)2 )
carrying o(1) of the sieved main term.

Proposition 9.12 (Cofactor factorization obstructions: prime cofactor, two-prime cofactor crite-
rion, and simple-factor extraction). Let n ≡ 1 (mod 24) be prime, c ≡ 3 (mod 4) with gcd(c, n) =
1, and for gcd(K, c) = 1 write Dc (K) := {de−1 mod c : d, e | K}. If gcd(U, V ) = 1 and
gcd(U V, c) = 1, divisor separation and inversion invariance give

                                                                                           (162)
                                                                             
       Dc (U V ) = Dc (U ) Dc (V ),    −1 ∈ Dc (U V ) ⇐⇒ Dc (U ) ∩ − Dc (V ) ̸= ∅,

whereas without factoring V , the classes {1, V, V −1 } provide only a subset of Dc (V ). Specifically:


                                                  90
 (a) (Prime cofactor obstruction). Suppose KI (c) = (nc + 1)/4 = U ℓ, where ℓ is prime and
     gcd(U, ℓ) = 1. If c > 4U 2 + 1, then −1 ∈   / Dc (KI (c)). This is an exact archimedean
     obstruction for a prime cofactor at c > 4U 2 + 1, rather than a general parity barrier for
     arbitrary sieves.

 (b) (Two-prime cofactor three-branch criterion). Let KI (p) = U ℓr (p ≡ 3 (mod 4) prime),
     where ℓ, r are primes (possibly equal), gcd(U, ℓr) = 1, and p > 4U 2 + 1. Setting A := Dp (U ),
     one has

            −1 ∈ Dp KI (p) ⇐⇒ ℓ ∈ −A or ℓ ∈ −(4U )−1 A or 4U ℓ2 ∈ −A.                        (163)
                             

                                                                                       
                                                                                        q
 (c) (Six or eight admissible residue classes). In (b), if U = q is prime with          p   = −1 and
      p > 4q 2 + 1, the number of residue classes of ℓ (mod p) permitted by        (163) is exactly 8,
      unless p | (4q 3 + 1), in which case it is 6.

 (d) (Exact encounter decomposition after extracting a simple factor). If q ∥ K (gcd(K, p) = 1),
     set V := K/q and C(r) := #{(u, v) : u, v | V, u/v ≡ r (mod p)}. Then the number of
     encounters Rp (K) = #{(d, e) : d, e | K, d + e ≡ 0 (mod p)} satisfies

                                      Rp (K) = 2 C(−1) + 2 C(−q).
                   
      Even when pq = −1, positivity of C(−1) + C(−q) is not automatic: the subgroup generated
      by prime factors and the set of subset products with bounded exponents are distinct objects
      (for instance, in Z/(2m)Z with m odd, k < m copies of the generator 1 generate the full
      group while their signed sums in {−k, . . . , k} never reach m).

Proof. (a) We have Dc (KI (c)) = Dc (U ){1, ℓ, ℓ−1 } and ℓ−1 ≡ 4U (mod c). Any quotient of divisors
of U writes in lowest terms as u/v with gcd(u, v) = 1 and uv | U . The first branch u/v ≡ −1
(mod c) would require c | (u + v), whereas 0 < u + v ≤ uv + 1 ≤ U + 1 < c. A translated
branch would require, up to inversion, c | (u + 4U v), whereas (u − 1)(4U v − 1) ≥ 0 implies
0 < u + 4U v ≤ 4U uv + 1 ≤ 4U 2 + 1 < c.
    (b) The set of divisor quotients of KI (p) = U ℓr expands as A 1, ℓ±1 , r±1 , (ℓr)±1 , ℓ/r, r/ℓ ,
                                                                     

which remains valid when ℓ = r upon accounting for repetitions. Since ℓr ≡ (4U )−1 (mod p), the
branches A and A(ℓr)±1 are excluded by the exact same archimedean inequalities as in (a). The
ℓ±1 -branches give ℓ ∈ −A, the r±1 -branches give ℓ ∈ −(4U )−1 A, and the (ℓ/r)±1 -branches give
4U ℓ2 ∈ −A by inversion invariance
                                 of A.
    (c) For U = q prime with pq = −1, we have A = {1, q, q −1 }. The two linear branches
in (163) are {−1, −q, −q −1 } and {−(4q)−1 , −4−1 , −(4q 2 )−1 }. Within each triple, the three classes
are distinct since 1, q, q 2 are distinct modulo p. Any overlap between the two triples would force
4q a ≡ 1 (mod p) for some a ∈ {−1, 0, 1, 2, 3}: a = −1 would force   q = 4 (impossible for a prime),
a ∈ {0, 1, 2} is excluded by p > 4q + 1, and a = 3 is excluded by pq = −1. Thus the six linear
                                       2

classes are distinct. In the quadratic branch 4qℓ2 ∈ {−1,
                                                       −q, −q }, only −1 ∈ −A has quadratic
                                                               −1

residue right-hand side (−4q)−1 modulo p (since −1  p   = −1 = pq ), yielding two roots ℓ (mod p)
of 4qℓ2 ≡ −1 (mod p). Substituting the six linear classes into 4qℓ2 ≡ −1 (mod p) shows that an
intersection occurs if and only if 4q 3 ≡ −1 (mod p), in which case the two roots are ±q, both
already present in the linear branches.
    (d) Every divisor of K = qV (q ∤ V ) is of the form u or qu with u | V . The four pair choices
yield Rp (K) = 2C(−1) + C(−q) + C(−q −1 ), and exchanging u and v gives C(−q −1 ) = C(−q).

Proof. A Type II encounter at shift p produces a Type II decomposition with K = (n + p)/4.

dividing by p − 1 ≫ H = nθ yields (166).

9.5   Global synthesis and conditional finiteness
We conclude Section 9 by assembling the Growing-Depth Container Hierarchy of Sections 3–7 and
the Transversal Reciprocity and Bohr–Siegel Cross-Polytope Architecture of Subsections 9.1–9.4
into our main global synthesis theorem.

Theorem 9.14 (Main Global Synthesis Theorem: Exact Criteria, Ambient Factorial Volume
Bounds, and Conditional Finiteness for n ≡ 1 (mod 24)). Let N = {n prime : n ≡ 1 (mod 24)},
and let E := {n ∈ N : 4/n = 1/x + 1/y + 1/z has no solution in N∗3 } denote the global excep-
tional set of the Erdős–Straus conjecture on N . Combining the Growing-Depth Container Hierar-
chy (Sections 3–7) with the Transversal Reciprocity and Bohr–Siegel Cross-Polytope Architecture
(Theorems 9.4–9.10) yields the following global synthesis:

 (a) (Unconditional global structured density at the Shannon–Bombieri CRT ceiling and uncon-
     ditional 5/7 exponent on the full exceptional set). Across all primes n ≤ N with n ≡ 1
     (mod 24), the unconditional two-parameter Type II container sieve at adaptive depth J(N )

(b) (Exact criteria and ambient volume bounds). Fix A > 5 and ε ∈ (0, 1/6), and set H :=
    1        A
    4 (log n) . For every prime n ≡ 1 (mod 24):

     (i) Least non-residue prime shift: Choose a non-residue a (mod n) and a reduced class b

Remark 9.15 (Scope of the unconditional density bounds, the explicit threshold N0 , and the
conditional threshold N∗ ). Three concluding clarifications summarize the logical architecture of
Theorem 9.14:

 (1) Density bounds vs. finiteness in part (a): The unconditional bound (168) controls the density
     of exceptions up to N , but its right-hand side tends to infinity with N and thus does not
     by itself imply finiteness of E.

 (2) Ambient threshold N0 vs. arithmetic positivity in part (b): The factorial inequality (170) de-
     fines an explicit threshold N0 (A, ε) for the ambient counting measure Mobs (p; n) < 1. How-
     ever, as χp varies over Gb − , the phase tuple (χp (q1 ), . . . , χp (qk )) traverses the deterministic
                                p
     image of Gb−p rather  than  independent uniform coordinates in (µ−          2dp ) , and the Bohr–Siegel
                                                                                      k

     inequality (143) is a sufficient certificate of failure whose negation requires wrap-around
     control—such as Hypothesis T or the Kneser saturation criterion (155)—to certify arith-
     metic positivity.

 (3) Non-bounding of NT by N0 in part (c): While Hypothesis T as stated implies finiteness of
     E with N∗ = NT , the factorial crossing inequality (170) for N0 does not furnish an upper
     bound for NT .

9.6    Concluding remarks
Taken together with the companion paper [1], the results established here bridge the gap between
fixed-depth divisor sieves and growing-depth large sieves for the Erdős–Straus conjecture, and
delineate both the unconditional power of composite witnesses and the exact spectral frontier
separating density bounds from finiteness.
     First, on the algebraic and additive-combinatorial side (Sections 2–6), replacing subgroup

A     Technical Appendix: Algebraic Reconstruction, Sieved Can-
      didate Abundance, Square-Divisor Certificates, and Positivity
      Obstructions
In this appendix, we provide complete, self-contained proofs of the auxiliary results referenced in
Section 9. We proceed in three stages:

    • Algebraic reconstructions and fixed-parameter reductions (Subsections A.1–A.2): exact unit-
      fraction reconstruction from the difference sets Dc (Kj (c)), factor reciprocity, diophantine
      coupling between Type I and Type II, and finite reductions at fixed parameter α ≥ 1.

    • Unconditional candidate abundance and divergent sieved main terms (Subsections A.3–A.5):
      harmonic mass of square-free non-residue parameters α, Rosser–Iwaniec linear sieving of
      prime shifts p ≡ 3 (mod 4) to annihilate all odd character orders < 2z, and divergence of
      the divisor-weighted square-free main term Msfj .




                                                 96
   • Square-divisor positivity certificates and open spectral error targets (Subsections A.6–A.7):
     complete finite positivity criteria indexed by square divisors b | h2 , and the precise formula-
     tion of the open signed spectral error bounds (C17) and (C76).

Throughout this appendix, unless stated otherwise, n denotes a prime satisfying n ≡ 1 (mod 24).

A.1    Algebraic core and factor reciprocity for KI (c) and KII (c)
We begin by recording the exact algebraic correspondence between the multiplicative difference
sets of the shifted integers KI (c) and KII (c) and unit-fraction representations of 4/n, together
with the quadratic reciprocity laws governing their prime factors.
    For c ≡ 3 (mod 4) with gcd(c, n) = 1, recall from Theorem 9.5 the two shift integers
                                          nc + 1                      n+c
                               KI (c) =          ,        KII (c) =       ,                        (172)

both of which are coprime to c. We write p when the shift c is prime; the inequality c < n
holds throughout all asymptotic applications to c ≍ nθ with θ < 1. For any integer K ≥ 1 with
gcd(K, c) = 1, we denote by

                             Dc (K) := de−1 mod c : d, e | K

the multiplicative difference set of positive divisors of K modulo c.

Proposition A.1 (Reconstruction). If −1 ∈ Dc (Kj (c)) for j ∈ {I, II}, then one can write

                        Kj (c) = αuv,         gcd(u, v) = 1,          u + v = ac

with positive integers a, α, u, v ≥ 1, yielding the explicit unit-fraction identities
                    4     1        1    1
                      =       +      +                                        (j = I),             (173)
                    n   nαuv     aαu   aαv
                    4    1        1      1
                      =      +       +                                        (j = II).            (174)
                    n   αuv     aαun   aαvn
Conversely, any factorization Kj (c) = αuv with c | (u + v) implies −1 ∈ Dc (Kj (c)) and yields the
corresponding decomposition (173) or (174), even without assuming gcd(u, v) = 1.

Proof. Suppose d, e | Kj (c) satisfy c | (d + e), and set g = gcd(d, e). Since gcd(g, c) = 1, the
coprime positive integers u = d/g and v = e/g satisfy c | (u + v) and divide Kj (c), whence
uv | Kj (c). Defining α = Kj (c)/(uv) and a = (u + v)/c, we observe that the sum of the last
two unit fractions in (173) equals aαuv
                                    u+v
                                         = KIc(c) , while in (174) it equals nKIIc (c) . Substituting the
definitions (172) immediately gives (173) and (174). The converse follows by the same algebraic
identity without dividing by gcd(u, v).

Remark A.2 (Parametrization convention). Proposition A.1 associates two distinct shift integers
KI (c) and KII (c) to the same target residue −1 mod c. By contrast, Theorem 9.4(a) works with the
single integer Kc = (n + c)/4 = KII (c) and distinguishes the two types via the targets −1 mod c
(Type II) and −n−1 mod c (Type I). The two conventions should be kept distinct unless the
corresponding change of variables is performed.

Remark A.7 (Disjointness of non-residue prime factors versus statistical independence). Propo-

                                                                                        

This arithmetic disjointness of non-residue prime factors should not, however, be conflated with
statistical independence of the factorizations of KI (c) and KII (c) as c varies.

A.2    Fixed-parameter finite reductions and the divisor encounter problem
Before varying the parameter α across polynomial ranges in Subsection A.3, we examine the
structure of the Type I and Type II witness equations when α ≥ 1 is held fixed. Doing so
yields an explicit finite reduction for fixed (n, α) and clarifies why varying α (or c) is essential in
asymptotic arguments.

Proposition A.8 (Exact finite reduction in Type I at fixed α). Fix n and α ≥ 1. The Type I
solutions
                      4αde = nc + 1,        d + e = ac,       d≤e
are in bijection with pairs (k, d) of positive integers satisfying
                           jnk                           
                                                     2n
                                  +1 ≤ k ≤                  ,      d | k,                        (177)
                             4α                    4α − 1
                        s := 4αk − n | (4αd2 + 1),          (4αk − 2n)d2 ≤ k,

via the explicit reconstruction a = k/d, c = (4αd2 + 1)/s, and e = ac − d.

Proof. Setting k = ad and substituting e = ac−d into 4αde = nc+1 yields (4αk −n)c = 4αd2 +1,
so s = 4αk−n > 0 and s | (4αd2 +1). The ordering condition e ≥ d is equivalent to ac ≥ 2d, which
upon multiplying by sd = dk/a becomes (4αk − 2n)d2 ≤ k. In particular, 4αk − 2n ≤ k/d2 ≤ k,
which gives (4α − 1)k ≤ 2n and establishes the upper bound on k in (177). Conversely, any pair
(k, d) satisfying (177) defines positive integers a = k/d, c = (4αd2 + 1)/s, and e = ac − d ≥ d ≥ 1
satisfying the initial system, with c ≡ 3 (mod 4) since s ≡ −n ≡ 3 (mod 4).

Corollary A.9 (Quantifiers of the fixed-α finiteness obstruction). If n | α, reducing 4αde = nc+1
modulo n shows that there is no Type I solution. When gcd(n, α) = 1, the modular inverse n−1
(mod 4α) exists and only finitely many shifts c can yield a Type I solution at parameter α: with
U = ⌊2n/(4α − 1)⌋, every such shift satisfies c ≤ (4αU 2 + 1)/3. Consequently, among primes p in
the reduced progression p ≡ −n−1 (mod 4α), the proportion of successful shifts tends to zero as
the upper cutoff on p tends to infinity at fixed n and α.

Proof. In (177), we have d ≤ k ≤ U and s = 4αk − n ≥ 3 (since s > 0 and s ≡ 3 (mod 4)),
which forces c = (4αd2 + 1)/s ≤ (4αU 2 + 1)/3. By Dirichlet’s theorem, the reduced progression
p ≡ −n−1 (mod 4α) contains infinitely many primes, whereas the number of successful shifts is
bounded for fixed (n, α).

Remark A.10 (Fixed-parameter asymptotics versus the joint regime n → ∞). The limiting
density zero in Corollary A.9 holds when (n, α) is fixed and the upper bound on p tends to +∞.
It says nothing by itself about the joint regime n → ∞ with p ≤ nθ (0 < θ < 1), where U ≍ n/α
grows with n; extrapolating the fixed-(n, α) limit to that regime without uniformity in n would
be a quantifier reversal.

Proposition A.11 (Finite reduction in Type II and square classes). For fixed α ≥ 1, the Type II
solutions 4αde = n + c, d + e = ac with d ≤ e are in bijection with pairs (d, e) of positive integers
satisfying
                              j n k                             
        2
                                                          n+d
   4αd − 2d ≤ n,        max d,          +1 ≤ e ≤                    ,     (4αde − n) | (d + e).
                                  4αd                     4αd − 1

                                                   99
Moreover, in both Type I and Type II, any solution at a non-square-free parameter α = βh2 induces
a solution at the square-free core β via the scaling (a, d, e) 7→ (ha, hd, he).
Proof. Setting c = 4αde−n > 0 and d+e = ac requires c | (d+e) and in particular c ≤ d+e, which
rearranges to (4αd − 1)e ≤ n + d. Combining this upper bound with e ≥ d yields 4αd2 − 2d ≤ n
and the stated interval for e; the converse is immediate by setting a = (d + e)/c. Finally, replacing
(α, a, d, e) by (β, ha, hd, he) preserves β(hd)(he) = αde and multiplies both d + e and a by h,
leaving c unchanged.

Proposition A.12 (Exact divisor-encounter formulation at fixed α). Let p ≡ −n−1 (mod 4α) be
prime and set V := (np + 1)/(4α) ∈ N. A Type I solution at parameter α and shift p exists if and
only if
                             ∃d | V :    4αd2 ≡ −1 (mod p).                                (178)
If p = p0 + 4αt, then V = V0 + nt. In the special case where V is prime, (178) is equivalent to
p | (4α + 1).
Proof. Writing e = V /d, the definition 4αV = np + 1 implies 4αV ≡ 1 (mod p). Consequently,
4αd2 ≡ −1 (mod p) holds if and only if d2 ≡ −V = −de (mod p), which (since gcd(d, p) = 1)
is equivalent to d + e ≡ 0 (mod p); Proposition A.1 then gives the correspondence with Type I
solutions. When V is prime, its only positive divisors are d = 1 and d = V , both of which
reduce (178) to the congruence 4α + 1 ≡ 0 (mod p).

Remark A.13 (Ambient quadratic solvability versus divisor encounter). Proposition A.12 un-
derscores a key structural distinction: the fact that the quadratic congruence 4αx2≡ −1 (mod p)
is solvable in the ambient group (Z/pZ)× (which holds automatically whenever αp = −1 and
p ≡ 3 (mod 4)) does not imply that either of its two roots modulo p is hit by an actual divisor
d|V.

A.3    Analytic inputs and abundance of candidate parameters
To move beyond fixed α and build an abundant supply of candidate shifts as n → ∞, we now
vary α over square-free quadratic non-residues modulo n and combine three unconditional results
from analytic number theory.

Remark A.17. By a theorem of Pollack [25], there are also at least nγ distinct non-residue primes
q ≤ nβ0 +ε for some γ > 0, which via Xylouris’s theorem yields a positive power of n distinct prime
shifts p ≤ nθ whenever θ > 5(β0 + ε). In Subsection A.4, we replace individual Linnik primes by
the Bombieri–Vinogradov theorem to obtain a positive proportion of all prime shifts p ∈ (2H, 4H].

A.4    Prime shifts, weighted Bombieri–Vinogradov, and annihilation of small
       character orders
We now pass from candidate existence to positive-density families of prime shifts p ∈ (2H, 4H]
(H = nθ ) for which Kj (p) is divisible to the first power by a non-residue prime q ≤ Z, and
we combine the Bombieri–Vinogradov theorem with the Rosser–Iwaniec linear sieve to annihilate
simultaneously all odd Dirichlet characters modulo p of order strictly less than 2z.

Lemma A.20 (Aggregated remainder control). Under (181), the linear-sieve remainders over
moduli u ≤ D, weighted by 3ω(d) over square-free d ≤ T and summed over one or two primes

                                           

                                 4DT Z 2 = 4H ρ+η+2b ≤ H 1/2−ε                                        (185)

for a fixed ε > 0 and all sufficiently large n. Grouping the terms according to the actual least-
common-multiple modulus m = lcm(4, u, d, q a ) (a ∈ {1, 2}) or m = lcm(4, u, d, q, r), we observe

Consequently, the total weight of compatible representations of a given modulus m, including the
factor 3ω(d) , is bounded by τ (m)C for an absolute constant C (while incompatible congruence
systems contribute zero). Applying Lemma A.18 then absorbs the aggregated remainder into
OA (H(log H)−A ).

A.6    Complete positivity criteria via square divisors b | h2
Whereas Subsections A.1–A.5 index decompositions by the shift c ≡ 3 (mod 4) and the divisors
of Kj (c), one may dually change the order of summation and index directly over an auxiliary
parameter h and a square divisor b | h2 (compare [5, §2]). We record here the resulting explicit
certificates and the complete finite positivity equivalence for prime n ≡ 1 (mod 24).

Proposition A.24 (Explicit square-divisor certificates). Let n ≡ 1 (mod 24) be prime.

  (i) (Type II). If h ≥ 1, b | h2 , and (4h − 1) | (n + 4b), set c := 4h−1
                                                                      n+4b

      Then c ≡ 3 (mod 4), K = (n + c)/4 > 0, b | hK, and
                                      4   1    1     1
                                        =   +    +       .                                      (194)
                                      n   K   hn   hKn/b

 (ii) (Type I). If h > n/4, b | h2 , and (4h − n) | (4b + 1), set c := 4h−n
                                                                       4b+1                   nb+h
                                                                            and K := hc − b = 4h−n .
      Then c ≡ 3 (mod 4), K = (nc + 1)/4 > 0, b | hK, and
                                       4    1   1    1
                                         =    +   +      .                                      (195)
                                       n   nK   h   hK/b

Proof. In both cases, the explicit expressions for c and K show that they are positive integers
with c ≡ 3 (mod 4), and the relation K = hc − b together with b | h2 implies b | hK. The linear
identities h(4K − n) = K + b in case (i) and h(4K − 1) = n(K + b) in case (ii) then verify (194)
and (195) immediately upon dividing by hKn.

Theorem A.25 (Complete positivity criterion for prime n). For a prime n ≡ 1 (mod 24), define
the two finite counting sums
                                        X        X
                            NII (n) :=              1 4h−1 | (n+4b) ,                  (196)
                                          1≤h≤(n−1)/6 b|h2
                                             X         X
                              NI (n) :=                       1 4h−n | (4b+1) .                 (197)
                                          n/4<h<n/2    b|h2

Then 4/n is a sum of three positive unit fractions if and only if NI (n) + NII (n) > 0.

Proof. The sufficiency of NI (n) + NII (n) > 0 follows directly from Proposition A.24. Conversely,
in any representation 4xyz = n(xy + xz + yz) for a prime n, either one or two of the denominators
x, y, z are multiples of n.
    In the two-multiple case (Type II), write x = K, y = nh, and z = nk with h ≤ k and n ∤ K.
Setting c = 4K − n > 0 gives chk = K(h + k), so b := ch − K = Kh    k is a positive integer satisfying
b ≤ K and gcd(b, c) = 1 (since any common divisor of b and c divides K and hence n, which is
coprime to c). Multiplying K = ch − b by h shows that b | hK = h2 c − hb, whence b | h2 since
gcd(b, c) = 1. Moreover, (4h − 1)c = 4(K + b) − c = n + 4b, and since c ≥ 3 and b ≤ K, we have
hc = K + b ≤ 2K = (n + c)/2, which yields h ≤ 2c    n
                                                              6 .
                                                      + 12 ≤ n−1
    In the one-multiple case (Type I), write x = nK, y = h, and z = k with h ≤ k and n ∤ hk. Then
c = (4K −1)/n ≡ 3 (mod 4) is a positive integer satisfying chk = K(h+k), so b = ch−K = Kh/k
is a positive divisor of h2 satisfying (4h − n)c = 4b + 1 > 0 (forcing h > n/4), and the inequality
hc = K + b ≤ 2K = (nc + 1)/2 implies h < n2 + 2c   1
                                                      < n/2 since n is odd and c ≥ 3.

A.7    Open signed spectral error targets
We conclude by recording the exact spectral error bounds that would be required to pass from the
unconditional main-term lower bounds of Section 9 and Subsection A.5 to a point-wise positivity
proof for individual primes n.

                                                 106
   In the notation of Theorem 9.5, a sufficient condition for the cumulative encounter count
R(n; nθ ) = M(n; nθ ) + E(n; nθ ) to be strictly positive for all primes n > N0 (n ≡ 1 (mod 24))
would be the signed error lower bound

                                     E(n; nθ ) ≥ −(κ0 θ − δ) log n                               (C17)

for some θ ∈ (0, 1) and δ ∈ (0, κ0 θ) (or E(n; nθ ) ≥ −o((log n)4 ) in view of (134)). Similarly, in the
sifted square-free shift family Gj of Theorem A.22, writing

                       p∈Gj

with the odd-character spectral error

                        Ejsf :=                      sf
                                              χ(−1) Fp,j (χ) − mj (p) ,                           (198)

                                                 (log log n)3/2

    Neither (C17), nor (C76), nor the Transversal Hypothesis T is implied by the unconditional
main-term lower bounds (134) and (187): as demonstrated by the exact small-LCM divisor-
pair cancellation of Theorem 9.5(c) and the archimedean support restriction of Proposition 9.13,
annihilating odd characters of order < 2z via Bombieri–Vinogradov at moduli ≤ H 1/2−ε does not
by itself control the signed contribution of characters of order ≥ 2z at the final encounter modulus
p ≍ H.
